19:T3e69,<blockquote>
<p>Understanding where protocol ends and intelligence begins in modern AI agent architectures.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>MCP (Model Context Protocol) and agentic skills are not competing concepts — they are complementary layers. <strong>MCP is the standardised connectivity layer</strong> that defines <em>how</em> an agent reaches external tools and data. <strong>Skills are the domain-specific capability modules</strong> that define <em>what</em> an agent knows how to do. The most effective agent architectures combine both: skills for intelligent orchestration, MCP for universal tool access. If you are building AI agents and treating these as either/or, you’re leaving architecture on the table.</p>
<p>To make this concrete, we’ll use a single running example throughout: a <strong>dbt MCP server</strong> and a <strong>Databricks MCP server</strong> as the connectivity layer, and a custom <strong>Pipeline Triage Skill</strong> as the intelligence layer that orchestrates across both. By the end, you’ll see exactly where protocol ends and domain logic begins.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*1_xKp0Ort7imo9hIcfnZBg.png" alt=""></p>
<p>Skills define capability · MCP defines connectivity · Together they form the agent stack</p>
<h3>Overview</h3>
<p>If you’ve spent any time in the AI agent space recently, you’ve probably encountered two concepts that keep showing up in the same conversations but that few people clearly distinguish: <strong>MCP servers</strong> and <strong>agentic skills</strong>. Both relate to how AI agents interact with the outside world, but they operate at fundamentally different levels of the stack.</p>
<p>This article is for data engineers, AI engineers, and platform engineers who are building or evaluating agent architectures and want a clear mental model for where each concept fits. Whether you’re wiring up a dbt project to an LLM or designing a multi-tool agent that triages overnight pipeline failures across Databricks, dbt, and Slack — understanding the MCP and agent skills boundary will sharpen your design decisions.</p>
<p>We’ll start by defining MCP using the <strong>dbt MCP server</strong> and <strong>Databricks MCP server</strong> as concrete examples, then introduce the <strong>Pipeline Triage Skill</strong> to show what skills add on top. From there, we’ll explore how they layer together in a real architecture, compare them side by side, and close with practical guidance for building systems that treat both as first-class citizens.</p>
<h3>What Is MCP?</h3>
<p><strong>Model Context Protocol (MCP)</strong> is Anthropic’s open protocol for connecting AI models to external tools, data sources, and APIs through a standardised client-server interface. Think of it as the <strong>USB-C for AI</strong> — a universal plug that lets any model talk to any tool without needing custom integration code for each combination.</p>
<p>Before MCP, if you wanted to connect an LLM to your dbt project, you had to write bespoke API glue: authentication handling, request formatting, response parsing, error management. Want to also connect it to Databricks? That meant another round of custom integration code. Multiply that by every tool your agent needs, and you get a combinatorial explosion of integration code — fragile, duplicated, and hard to maintain.</p>
<p>MCP solves this with a clean architectural separation. An <strong>MCP server</strong> wraps an external tool or data source and exposes its capabilities through a standardised interface. An <strong>MCP client</strong> lives inside the agent or model runtime, speaks the protocol, and routes tool calls to the appropriate server. The model never needs to know the implementation details of the tool — it just knows what capabilities are available and how to invoke them.</p>
<p>Let’s ground this with our two examples:</p>
<p>The <strong>dbt MCP server</strong> wraps your dbt project and exposes operations like dbt run, dbt test, listing resources, and parsing DAG lineage — all through the standard MCP interface. Any MCP-compatible agent can discover and invoke these operations without knowing anything about dbt&#39;s CLI, manifest files, or project structure.</p>
<p>The <strong>Databricks MCP server</strong> does the same for your Databricks workspace — exposing capabilities like querying system.lakeflow tables for job run history, checking cluster status, or reading data freshness metadata. Again, the agent doesn&#39;t need to know about Databricks REST APIs or authentication flows.</p>
<p>This is a protocol-level concern. Neither MCP server knows or cares <em>why</em> you’re querying dbt logs or Databricks system tables, or <em>what</em> you plan to do with the results. They handle the plumbing: discovery, authentication, invocation, and response formatting. As we’ll see in the architecture section below, this makes MCP the foundation layer that everything else builds on.</p>
<p>But here’s the critical question: if an overnight pipeline fails and your agent can reach both dbt and Databricks via MCP, does it automatically know how to <em>diagnose</em> the failure? That’s where skills come in.</p>
<h3>What Are Agentic Skills?</h3>
<p>If MCP is the I/O layer, <strong>agentic skills</strong> are the applications that run on top of it. A skill is a <strong>self-contained capability module</strong> that encapsulates domain knowledge, prompt templates, reasoning chains, and orchestration logic for a specific task.</p>
<p>Here’s the analogy: MCP is the operating system’s I/O subsystem — it handles file systems, network sockets, and device drivers. Skills are the apps you install on top. The apps depend on the I/O layer to read and write data, but they own the intelligence of <em>what to do</em> with that data.</p>
<p>Let’s make this real with our <strong>Pipeline Triage Skill</strong>. Imagine your daily customer churn model fails overnight. Without a skill, your agent has raw access to dbt and Databricks via their MCP servers — it could retrieve run logs, query system tables, or list failed tests. But it doesn’t inherently know the <em>workflow</em> of triaging a pipeline failure. It doesn’t know to:</p>
<ul>
<li>First check dbt run logs to identify <em>which</em> models and tests failed.</li>
<li>Then correlate those failures against Databricks system.lakeflow tables to check whether upstream jobs ran late or produced anomalous row counts.</li>
<li>Then synthesise these signals into a root-cause hypothesis — “the source table arrived 3 hours late, causing a freshness test to trip and cascade downstream.”</li>
<li>Then format a structured incident summary and post it to the team’s #data-incidents Slack channel with root cause, impacted models, and a recommended re-run sequence.</li>
</ul>
<p>The <strong>Pipeline Triage Skill</strong> encapsulates that entire reasoning chain — the sequencing, the cross-tool correlation, the domain judgement about what constitutes a root cause, the knowledge of how your team handles incidents. It’s a workflow, not a single tool call. The skill <em>uses</em> the dbt and Databricks MCP servers under the hood, but the skill owns the intelligence.</p>
<p>The architecture section below formalises this distinction between raw tool access (MCP) and orchestrated domain logic (skills).</p>
<h3>Architecture: How MCP and Skills Layer Together</h3>
<p>Here’s where the relationship becomes concrete. In a well-designed agent architecture, MCP and skills don’t sit side by side — they stack vertically. Let’s trace our Pipeline Triage example through the full stack:</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*dm_RSjy6kPOCjZMgDznrmw.png" alt=""></p>
<p>image created by author</p>
<p><strong>What you are seeing:</strong> the user asks the agent to investigate a pipeline failure. The agent delegates to the Pipeline Triage Skill, which orchestrates a multi-step diagnosis. At each step, the skill calls the MCP client, which routes the request to the appropriate MCP server — dbt for run logs, Databricks for system table queries, Slack for the incident post. Each MCP server executes one stateless API interaction. The skill owns the reasoning across all of them.</p>
<p><strong>The key takeaway:</strong> skills consume MCP — not the other way around. The Pipeline Triage Skill makes three MCP calls during its execution, but MCP never invokes the skill. This directionality is what makes the architecture clean: MCP handles the <strong>horizontal breadth</strong> of tool connectivity (how many tools can I reach?), while skills handle the <strong>vertical depth</strong> of task-specific intelligence (what do I do once I get there?).</p>
<p>Notice something important: the dbt MCP server and Databricks MCP server know nothing about each other. Neither knows that another workflow step called the other. Only the Pipeline Triage Skill holds the full picture — it orchestrates the individual MCP calls and chains them into a coherent diagnosis. The comparison table in the next section formalises this layering.</p>
<h3>MCP vs Skills: Side-by-Side Comparison</h3>
<p>To crystallise the distinction, here’s how MCP and skills compare across the dimensions that matter most when you’re making architecture decisions. We’ll use our running example to ground each row:</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/969/1*l5bWM4oXo7Tgdpc7En85wQ.png" alt=""></p>
<p>Notice the pattern: every dimension maps to the same fundamental split between <strong>connectivity</strong> and <strong>capability</strong>. The dbt MCP server answers “can my agent reach dbt?” The Pipeline Triage Skill answers “does my agent know what to do when a dbt run fails?”</p>
<p>This comparison also highlights where teams sometimes get confused — the overlap section below digs into that.</p>
<h3>Where They Overlap — And Where They Don’t</h3>
<p>The confusion between MCP and skills makes sense because <strong>skills use MCP servers under the hood</strong>, which hides the boundary from the outside. When a user says “check why the churn model failed last night”, it looks like a single operation. But architecturally, two layers do the work: the Pipeline Triage Skill decides <em>which</em> logs to pull and <em>how</em> to interpret failure patterns, while the dbt MCP server executes the actual API call to retrieve run results.</p>
<p><strong>The overlap zone</strong> is where a skill wraps a single MCP server call with minimal additional logic. Imagine a simple skill that takes a natural-language question like “which dbt models failed today?”, forwards it to the dbt MCP server, and returns the raw result. That’s thin enough to feel redundant — the agent could call the MCP server directly.</p>
<p>So when do you actually need a skill? The answer lies in our Pipeline Triage example.</p>
<p><strong>Skills diverge from MCP</strong> when the task requires <strong>multi-step reasoning</strong>, <strong>cross-tool orchestration</strong>, or <strong>domain-specific judgement</strong>. The dbt MCP server can execute dbt test and return logs — but it has no idea what those failures <em>mean</em>. It doesn&#39;t know that a freshness test failure on stg_customer_events should trigger a check against Databricks system.lakeflow tables to see whether the upstream ingestion job ran late. It doesn&#39;t know that your team classifies this as a Severity 3 incident. It doesn&#39;t know to post to #data-incidents rather than #data-general. The skill — not the MCP server — owns all of that orchestration and domain judgement.</p>
<p>The practical rule of thumb, which connects back to the architecture diagram above: <strong>if it’s a single tool call, MCP alone may suffice. If it involves judgement, sequencing, or synthesis across tools, you want a skill.</strong></p>
<h3>So What?</h3>
<p>Now that the MCP-skills mental model is clear, here’s what to do with it:</p>
<p><strong>1. Design your agent architecture in layers.</strong> Treat MCP as your connectivity tier and skills as your application tier. Don’t embed triage logic in your dbt MCP server, and don’t hardcode Databricks API calls inside your Pipeline Triage Skill. Clean separation lets you swap tools without rewriting workflows — replace Databricks with Snowflake, and only the MCP server changes; the skill’s reasoning logic stays intact.</p>
<p><strong>2. Start with MCP, add skills as complexity grows.</strong> For simple, single-tool interactions — “run dbt test and show me the results&quot; — connecting the dbt MCP server directly to your agent is perfectly fine. Introduce a skill layer when you find yourself writing multi-step prompts, cross-tool orchestration, or conditional logic that the model shouldn&#39;t have to figure out from scratch every time.</p>
<p><strong>3. Invest in your MCP server catalogue.</strong> The value of skills multiplies with the breadth of your MCP connectivity. Our Pipeline Triage Skill is powerful because it can orchestrate across dbt, Databricks, <em>and</em> Slack. Add a GitHub MCP server, and the same skill could auto-create an issue for the on-call engineer. Treat your MCP server catalogue like an API gateway — the more standardised endpoints you expose, the more sophisticated the skills you can build on top.</p>
<p><strong>4. Think complementary, not competitive.</strong> As the agent ecosystem matures, the teams that treat MCP and skills as complementary layers — not as an either/or choice — will build the most robust and maintainable AI systems. MCP gives you the universal reach. Skills give you the domain intelligence. Together, they form the foundation of production-grade agentic architecture.</p>

<p><em>This article was originally published at <a href="https://medium.com/towards-data-engineering/mcp-vs-agentic-skills-whats-the-difference-and-how-do-they-work-together-0ddaea01f73c?source=rss-670f6306e3c0------2">https://medium.com/towards-data-engineering/mcp-vs-agentic-skills-whats-the-difference-and-how-do-they-work-together-0ddaea01f73c?source=rss-670f6306e3c0------2</a></em></p>
1a:T1ce9,<blockquote>
<p>A “Software Engineering” mindset for data, without the infrastructure tax.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>The landscape of Databricks automation is shifting. With the release of <strong>Databricks CLI v0.279.0</strong>, a new era of “Direct Deployment” has arrived, signalling the eventual retirement of the Terraform-based backend for asset bundles.</p>
<p>Databricks is deprecating the Terraform engine and will require the Direct Deployment Engine as the <strong>sole supported path in 2026</strong>. If you’ve struggled with registry connection errors in locked-down environments or found Terraform state management too heavy for simple ETL pipelines, this change is for you.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*1vVtx7puXhu5vjjZR8yAOA.png" alt=""></p>
<p>self contained bundle</p>
<h3>What are Declarative Automation Bundles (DABs)?</h3>
<p>Declarative Automation Bundles – formerly Databricks Asset Bundles – are the recommended approach for packaging workspace resources like jobs, DLT pipelines, and notebooks. Think of them as <strong>Infrastructure-as-Code (IaC) tailored specifically for the analytics layer.</strong></p>
<p>A typical databricks.yml defines your resources simply:</p>
<pre><code>bundle:
  name: my_etl_pipeline
resources:
  jobs:
    daily_ingest:
      name: &quot;Daily Ingestion Job&quot;
      tasks:
        - task_key: ingest
          notebook_task:
            notebook_path: ./notebooks/ingest.py
targets:
  dev:
    workspace:
      host: https://my-dev-workspace.databricks.com
</code></pre><p>While the model is elegant, the underlying mechanism — Terraform — historically introduced significant friction for enterprise engineering teams.</p>
<h3>The Terraform Dependency: Where It Broke Down</h3>
<p>Until now, DABs acted as a wrapper for Terraform. Every bundle deploy trigger initiated a heavy sequence: downloading the Terraform binary, fetching the provider from registry.terraform.io, and generating internal state files.</p>
<p>For teams in <strong>regulated sectors like telecom, finance, or healthcare,</strong> this architecture created three primary headaches:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>The Air-Gap Problem:</strong> CI/CD agents in restricted zones often lack outbound internet access, leading to the dreaded Could not retrieve provider error.</li>
<li><strong>The Skills Gap:</strong> Data engineers shouldn’t need to be Terraform experts to deploy a notebook. Managing provider mirrors and complex state files adds unnecessary overhead.</li>
<li><strong>The Version Lag:</strong> Supporting new Databricks features meant waiting for the Terraform provider to update before those resources could be automated.</li>
</ul>
<h3>The Direct Deployment Engine: Architecture and Advantages</h3>
<p>The <strong>Direct Deployment Engine</strong> eliminates the middleman. Instead of generating Terraform config and shelling out to terraform apply, the CLI now performs direct CRUD operations against Databricks REST APIs using the native Go SDK.</p>
<p>The result is a <strong>self-contained binary</strong>. No Terraform download. No provider registry. No proxy issues.</p>
<h3>Adopting Direct Deployment</h3>
<h3>Option 1: New Bundles (Never Deployed Before)</h3>
<p>For fresh projects, opt-in by setting an environment variable or updating your configuration:</p>
<p><strong>Via Environment Variable:</strong></p>
<pre><code>export DATABRICKS_BUNDLE_ENGINE=direct 
databricks bundle deploy -t dev
</code></pre><p><strong>Via</strong> <strong>databricks.yml:</strong></p>
<pre><code>bundle:
  name: my_pipeline
  engine: direct  # Sets the default for all targets
</code></pre><h3>Option 2: Migrating Existing Bundles</h3>
<p>If you already have bundles deployed via Terraform, you must migrate your state file. The Direct Engine uses a new JSON schema (resources.json) rather than terraform.tfstate.</p>
<p><strong>Sync:</strong> Ensure your current environment is fully synced.</p>
<p>databricks bundle deploy -t my_target.</p>
<p><strong>Migrate:</strong> Convert the state file:</p>
<p>databricks bundle deployment migrate -t my_target.</p>
<p><strong>Verify:</strong> Confirm zero planned changes.</p>
<p>databricks bundle plan -t my_target.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Finalise:</strong> Deploy to lock in the new state.</li>
</ul>
<blockquote>
<p><em><strong>Note:</strong></em> <em>The migration command still needs to read the old Terraform state, so run this step in an environment with internet access before moving the bundle to a locked-down zone</em></p>
</blockquote>
<h3>Where Terraform Still Belongs</h3>
<p>This is not a deprecation of Terraform itself. Terraform remains the best tool for <strong>platform infrastructure</strong> — the foundation that sits beneath your workloads:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Compute and Networking:</strong> VPCs, subnets, and security groups.</li>
<li><strong>Workspace Provisioning:</strong> Creating the workspace and Unity Catalog metastores.</li>
<li><strong>Identity:</strong> Service principals, IAM roles, and group memberships.</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>The emerging best practice is clear:</strong> use Terraform for infrastructure provisioning and use DABs (with the direct engine) for analytics asset deployment. These are complementary layers, not competing tools.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>The transition from Terraform to direct deployment is a signal of where Databricks is headed: a streamlined developer experience that removes friction between your code and your workspace. For data engineering teams, the action items are clear: <strong>Evaluate the direct engine on non-production targets now</strong> to ensure your pipelines are future-proofed for 2026.</p>
</blockquote>

1b:T309c,<blockquote>
<p>LLMs are smart but blind. Your semantic layer is organised but silent. MCP is the bridge that lets AI see your data — and answer questions without hallucinating.</p>
</blockquote>
<h3>The Promise and the Problem of “Chat With Your Data”</h3>
<p>Every data platform now offers some version of the same pitch: “Ask questions about your data in natural language. No SQL required.”</p>
<p>The promise is compelling. A business user types “How did we perform last quarter?” and gets an accurate, well-formatted answer — no tickets, no waiting, no SQL expertise needed.</p>
<p>The reality, in most implementations, is far less reliable.</p>
<p>When a large language model generates SQL against your warehouse, it’s guessing. It guesses at table names based on column headers it can see. It guesses at join paths based on naming conventions. It guesses at how to aggregate, what filters to apply, what time grain to use, and how to handle nulls. Sometimes the guesses are right. Often they’re subtly wrong. And the failure mode is insidious — <strong>the LLM returns a number that looks plausible but is silently incorrect</strong>.</p>
<p>This isn’t a model intelligence problem. GPT-4, Claude, Gemini — they’re all sophisticated enough to write syntactically correct SQL. The problem is <strong>context</strong>. The LLM doesn’t know your organisation’s metric definitions, your business logic conventions, your join hierarchies, or your data quality rules. It’s working from column names and whatever patterns it absorbed during training.</p>
<p>That’s not enough for analytics you can trust.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*uJDWEdQ88GIyH_WcfYxtSQ.png" alt=""></p>
<h3>What’s Missing: Structured Context</h3>
<p>The gap between “AI that writes SQL” and “AI that gives trustworthy answers” is <strong>structured context – the governance layer</strong> that defines how your data is modelled, tested, and interpreted.</p>
<p>In the below articles,</p>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://aradsouza.medium.com/why-data-teams-need-a-semantic-layer-83947a5a0057">Why Data Teams Need a Semantic Layer</a></li>
<li><a href="https://aradsouza.medium.com/metrics-as-code-building-a-semantic-layer-with-dbt-and-metricflow-93d7e29e6ab3">Metrics as Code: Building a Semantic Layer With dbt and MetricFlow</a></li>
</ul>
<p>I covered how a <strong>semantic layer</strong> provides this governance for human consumers: metrics defined as code, version-controlled in Git, served consistently to every dashboard and report. If you’ve built a semantic layer with dbt and MetricFlow, you already have the hardest piece.</p>
<p>What’s been missing is the <strong>bridge – a</strong> way to make that governed context machine-readable so that AI agents can access the same definitions, the same metrics, and the same lineage that your human analysts use.</p>
<p>That’s what the <strong>dbt MCP Server</strong> provides.</p>
<h3>What Is MCP?</h3>
<p>The <strong>Model Context Protocol (MCP)</strong> is an open standard, originally released by Anthropic in late 2024, that defines how AI systems access external tools, data, and context at query time.</p>
<h4>A Simple Analogy</h4>
<p>Think of MCP as a <strong>USB-C port for AI</strong>. Before USB-C, every device had its own proprietary connector. You needed a different cable for every phone, camera, and laptop. USB-C standardised the interface — one port, universal compatibility.</p>
<p>MCP does the same for AI-to-data connections. Instead of building custom integrations for every AI tool — one connector for Claude, another for ChatGPT, another for Cursor, another for your internal copilot — you expose your data through a single MCP server. Any MCP-compatible client can plug in.</p>
<h4>How MCP Differs From Direct Database Access</h4>
<p>In a traditional “text-to-SQL” setup, the AI gets a database connection and writes SQL directly. This is powerful but ungoverned — there’s nothing between the LLM’s best guess and your production warehouse.</p>
<p>MCP works differently. Instead of raw database access, the AI gets a <strong>menu of tools</strong> — discrete, well-defined functions it can call, each with clear inputs and outputs. The AI selects the appropriate tool, passes in parameters, and gets back structured results.</p>
<p>This is fundamentally safer. The AI doesn’t have the freedom to write arbitrary SQL. It can only perform the operations you’ve exposed through tools. And because you control the tool definitions, you control the governance boundary.</p>
<h3>What the dbt MCP Server Exposes</h3>
<p>The dbt MCP Server takes everything in your dbt project — models, metrics, documentation, lineage, tests, semantic layer definitions — and wraps it in MCP tools that AI agents can call.</p>
<p>These tools fall into three categories, and understanding each is important for designing effective AI-over-data experiences.</p>
<h3>Discovery Tools: Letting AI Understand Your Data</h3>
<p>Before an AI agent can answer questions, it needs to understand what data exists and how it’s organised. Discovery tools provide this orientation:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Model exploration</strong> — list all models in the project, get details for specific models, explore mart-level models that are designed for consumption</li>
<li><strong>Lineage navigation</strong> — trace upstream dependencies (“where does this data come from?”) and downstream impacts (“what breaks if this table changes?”)</li>
<li><strong>Documentation access</strong> — read model descriptions, column descriptions, ownership metadata, freshness information</li>
<li><strong>Semantic search</strong> — find relevant models even when the user’s question doesn’t match an exact table or column name</li>
</ul>
<p>Think of this as giving a new analyst a thorough onboarding. Before answering a single question, the AI builds a contextual understanding of your data landscape — what exists, how it connects, who owns it, and what it means.</p>
<h3>Semantic Layer Tools: Querying Governed Metrics</h3>
<p>This is the critical differentiator. These tools connect directly to the governed metrics defined in your semantic layer:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>List metrics</strong> — discover what business metrics are available</li>
<li><strong>Get dimensions</strong> — understand how a given metric can be sliced (by time, region, product category, etc.)</li>
<li><strong>Get entities</strong> — understand the join keys and relationships available for a metric</li>
<li><strong>Query metrics</strong> — execute a metric query and return governed results</li>
<li><strong>Get compiled SQL</strong> — inspect the exact SQL that MetricFlow generates, without executing it</li>
</ul>
<p>When the AI calls query_metrics, it&#39;s not writing SQL. MetricFlow generates the SQL from your YAML definitions. The result is <strong>correct by construction</strong> — it&#39;s derived from the same definitions that power your dashboards.</p>
<p>This is the difference between an AI that hallucinates numbers and one that returns answers grounded in your source of truth.</p>
<h3>Execution Tools: Operating on Your dbt Project</h3>
<p>For development and operational workflows, the MCP server also exposes dbt CLI tools:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Build, run, test</strong> — execute models, run data tests, validate quality</li>
<li><strong>Compile</strong> — generate SQL from models without executing, useful for review and validation</li>
<li><strong>Parse</strong> — check project files for syntax correctness</li>
<li><strong>Show</strong> — execute ad-hoc SQL and return results</li>
<li><strong>Generate</strong> — scaffold new model YAML, source definitions, and staging models</li>
</ul>
<p>These tools enable a new class of use case: <strong>agentic development</strong>. AI agents that don’t just query your data but develop your dbt project — refactoring models, running tests, and validating changes against your existing governance framework.</p>
<h3>The Key Architectural Principle</h3>
<p>There’s one principle that makes the entire architecture work, and it’s worth stating explicitly:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>The LLM never writes raw SQL. It calls governed tools through MCP. The SQL is generated by MetricFlow from your governed definitions.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>In a typical “text-to-SQL” approach:</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*zF_KgOsSz81Mo1cU9zpYSw.png" alt=""></p>
<p>With the dbt MCP Server and semantic layer:</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*K1aQGafRJLyM2LfwSNsAlA.png" alt=""></p>
<p>The LLM’s job shifts from <strong>SQL generation</strong> (error-prone, ungoverned) to <strong>intent interpretation</strong> (what metric does the user want? what dimensions? what filters?) and <strong>tool selection</strong> (which MCP tool should I call?). These are tasks LLMs are genuinely good at.</p>
<p>The SQL generation — the part that requires deep knowledge of your schema, join paths, business logic, and platform-specific syntax — is handled by MetricFlow, which has that knowledge encoded in your YAML definitions.</p>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>This separation of concerns is why the architecture produces trustworthy results.</strong></p>
</blockquote>
<h3>Local vs. Remote: Two Deployment Models</h3>
<p>The dbt MCP Server supports two deployment modes, each suited to different use cases.</p>
<h3>Local MCP Server</h3>
<p>The local server runs on your machine alongside your dbt project. It provides full access to dbt CLI commands and works with dbt Core, the dbt CLI, and the dbt Fusion engine.</p>
<p><strong>Best for:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Individual development workflows with tools like Cursor, Claude, or VS Code</li>
<li>Full CLI access including build, run, test, and compile</li>
<li>Working with local dbt projects without requiring a cloud account</li>
<li>Proof-of-concept exploration and validation</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Trade-off:</strong> It’s tied to a single developer’s machine. Not suited for multi-user or production deployments.</p>
<h3>Remote MCP Server</h3>
<p>The remote server connects to the dbt platform via HTTP. No local installation required.</p>
<p><strong>Best for:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Production AI applications serving multiple users</li>
<li>Web applications and organisational-scale agent deployments</li>
<li>Consumption-focused use cases (querying metrics, exploring metadata, viewing lineage)</li>
<li>Environments where local software installation is restricted</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Trade-off:</strong> Primarily consumption-oriented. The full development CLI tools aren’t available through the remote server.</p>
<p>For most teams, the journey starts local (POC and development) and moves remote (production and organisational scale).</p>

<p><em>This article was originally published at <a href="https://medium.com/towards-data-engineering/the-dbt-mcp-server-how-the-model-context-protocol-bridges-governed-data-to-ai-agents-5ae25b7b6703?source=rss-670f6306e3c0------2">https://medium.com/towards-data-engineering/the-dbt-mcp-server-how-the-model-context-protocol-bridges-governed-data-to-ai-agents-5ae25b7b6703?source=rss-670f6306e3c0------2</a></em></p>
1c:T39b9,<blockquote>
<p>How to define business metrics once, version them in Git, and serve them consistently to every dashboard, notebook, and AI agent in your stack</p>
</blockquote>
<h3>The Idea in One Sentence</h3>
<p>A semantic layer lets you define business metrics — revenue, active users, conversion rate, whatever matters to your organisation — <strong>once, as code</strong>, and serve them consistently to every tool and consumer in your data stack.</p>
<p>That’s the pitch. This article is the practice. I’ll walk through the mechanics of building a semantic layer using dbt, MetricFlow, and semantic models – the actual components, how they fit together, and the decisions you’ll face along the way.</p>
<p>No vendor evangelism. Just the architecture and the trade-offs.</p>
<h3>Why Metrics Belong in the Transformation Layer</h3>
<p>If you have worked in data for any length of time, you have encountered the metric consistency problem. Revenue means one thing in the finance dashboard and another thing in the marketing report. Customer count includes trial users in one tool and excludes them in another. Everyone is technically “correct” — they’re just using different definitions.</p>
<p>The root cause is that most organisations define metrics at the <strong>BI layer</strong> — inside their visualisation tools. This means definitions are tool-specific, not version-controlled, and invisible to non-BI consumers like AI agents, notebooks, or APIs.</p>
<p>A semantic layer moves those definitions down into the <strong>transformation layer</strong> — the governed, testable, version-controlled layer where your data models already live. Metric definitions become YAML files in a Git repository, reviewed in pull requests, tested in CI/CD, and consumed identically by every downstream tool.</p>
<p>This isn’t just a tidy architectural principle. It has a concrete effect: <strong>change a metric definition once, and it updates everywhere.</strong> No more parallel maintenance across tools. No more “which number is right?” debates.</p>
<h3>The Three Building Blocks</h3>
<p>The dbt semantic layer is built on three interlocking components. Understanding how they relate is essential before writing any YAML.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*XjQA3WpUyMw1JGdMY1L4jg.png" alt=""></p>
<h3>1. Semantic Models — Mapping Business Concepts to Physical Data</h3>
<p>A semantic model is an abstraction layer that sits <strong>on top of</strong> an existing dbt model. It doesn’t replace your models or change your warehouse structure. Think of it as an annotation — it tells the query engine what your data <em>means</em> in business terms.</p>
<p>Every semantic model declares three types of metadata:</p>
<p><strong>Entities</strong> are the join keys. They define how tables connect to each other — the traversal paths in the semantic graph. If your orders table has a customer_id that links to a customers table, that&#39;s an entity. Entities are what MetricFlow uses to figure out join paths when constructing queries.</p>
<p>A practical analogy: entities are like the edges on a map. They don’t carry data themselves, but they define how you navigate from one place to another.</p>
<p><strong>Measures</strong> are the raw aggregations. A measure declares an aggregation function — sum, count, count_distinct, average, min, max — applied to a column in the underlying table. For example, sum(transaction_amount) on your orders model, or count_distinct(user_id) on your events model.</p>
<p>Measures are <strong>not</strong> metrics. They’re ingredients. A single measure might feed into multiple metrics. Think of measures as the atomic building blocks — they define what can be computed, not what should be reported.</p>
<p><strong>Dimensions</strong> are the attributes you slice by. Time dimensions (order date, signup month, fiscal quarter). Categorical dimensions (region, product category, subscription tier, channel). Any attribute that a business user might want to filter or group by.</p>
<p>Here’s the mental model: <strong>semantic models define what data you have and how it connects. Metrics define what questions you can ask.</strong></p>
<h4>An Everyday Analogy</h4>
<p>Imagine a recipe. The semantic model is like your pantry — it declares what ingredients you have (measures), how they’re organised (dimensions), and which pantries connect to which kitchens (entities). The metric is the recipe itself — it says “combine these ingredients in this way to produce this dish.” MetricFlow is the chef who reads the recipe and does the actual cooking.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*HnZbFHEff1UjZgKJ1aPrRQ.png" alt=""></p>
<h3>2. Metrics — Defining Business Logic as Composable Code</h3>
<p>Metrics are the business-facing definitions. They sit on top of measures and express the calculations stakeholders actually care about.</p>
<p>The dbt Semantic Layer supports four metric types, and they’re designed to be <strong>composable</strong> — each type can reference others, so you define logic once and recombine it freely.</p>
<p><strong>Simple metrics</strong> reference a single measure directly. Total revenue. Total signups. Distinct active users. These are your foundational building blocks — the metrics that map directly to a single aggregation.</p>
<p>When to use: any metric that’s a straight aggregation. “How many X do we have?” or “What’s the total Y?”</p>
<p><strong>Ratio metrics</strong> divide one metric by another. Revenue per user. Cost per acquisition. Defect rate. The numerator and denominator are each governed metrics themselves, so the ratio inherits their consistency.</p>
<p>When to use: any metric that’s one number divided by another. The key benefit is that if the numerator metric’s definition changes, the ratio automatically updates.</p>
<p><strong>Cumulative metrics</strong> compute running totals over time. Year-to-date revenue. 90-day rolling active users. Cumulative signups since launch. You define the time window and the grain; MetricFlow handles the window function SQL.</p>
<p>When to use: when business users need “as of” or “over the last N days” metrics. These are notoriously error-prone to implement by hand because window function logic varies by platform.</p>
<p><strong>Derived metrics</strong> are the most flexible type. They let you write arithmetic expressions that combine other metrics. Need (metric_a - metric_b) / metric_c? A derived metric handles it, and each component metric retains its own governance.</p>
<p>When to use: any metric that’s a formula combining other metrics. The power here is composability — you never duplicate the underlying logic.</p>
<h4>Why Composability Is the Key Insight</h4>
<p>The four types aren’t just categories. They’re a <strong>composition system</strong>. A ratio metric’s numerator can be a simple metric. A derived metric can reference a cumulative metric. This means you build a library of well-defined atomic metrics and then combine them into increasingly complex business calculations — without ever duplicating logic.</p>
<p>In practice, this changes how data teams think about metrics. Instead of building each new metric request from scratch (writing SQL, figuring out joins, handling edge cases), you compose it from existing, tested components. It’s the same shift that happened in software engineering when teams moved from monolithic scripts to modular, reusable functions.</p>
<h3>3. MetricFlow — The SQL Generation Engine</h3>
<p>MetricFlow is the engine that ties it all together. It reads your YAML definitions and translates metric requests into optimised SQL for your specific data platform.</p>
<p>Here’s what happens when someone (or something) asks for a metric:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Parse the request</strong> — “Give me monthly active users by region for the last 12 months”</li>
<li><strong>Resolve the metric</strong> — look up the YAML definition, identify which measures and semantic models are involved</li>
<li><strong>Plan the query</strong> — determine the optimal join path between the required tables, using entity relationships</li>
<li><strong>Generate SQL</strong> — construct platform-specific SQL (Redshift, Databricks, Snowflake, BigQuery, or others)</li>
<li><strong>Return or execute</strong> — either hand back the compiled SQL or execute it and return results</li>
</ol>
<p>The critical point: <strong>you never write the aggregation SQL yourself.</strong> MetricFlow generates it from your declarative definitions. This means the SQL is provably correct — it’s derived from governed definitions, not hand-written under time pressure.</p>
<p>MetricFlow is now open source under the Apache 2.0 licence and is being developed as part of the <strong>Open Semantic Interchange (OSI)</strong> initiative — a cross-vendor effort involving dbt Labs, Snowflake, Salesforce, Atlan, Alation, and others. The goal is vendor-neutral standards for semantic data exchange. Your metric definitions aren’t locked into any single platform.</p>
<h3>Where It Sits in the Stack</h3>
<p>A common question is where the semantic layer fits relative to the rest of the data platform. Here’s the mental model:</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*qVJHnGJwjH0S-OQSxc1Fyw.png" alt=""></p>
<p>Without the semantic layer, there’s a direct — and ungoverned — path from your data marts to each consumption tool. Each tool interprets the data on its own terms.</p>
<p>With the semantic layer, every consumer goes through a governed intermediary. The metrics, the joins, the aggregation logic are defined once and enforced everywhere.</p>
<h3>Practical Decisions You will Face</h3>
<h3>Where to Start</h3>
<p>Don’t try to model your entire warehouse on day one. Start with the <strong>most contested metrics</strong> — the ones that generate the most “is this number right?” conversations. These have the highest ROI because the trust problem is already visible.</p>
<p>A good first batch is 5–10 core metrics with the semantic models they depend on. Define them, validate against existing reports, ship them, and then expand.</p>
<h3>How Semantic Models Map to dbt Models</h3>
<p>Semantic models don’t require a one-to-one mapping with dbt models. You can create multiple semantic models from a single dbt model — useful when a wide fact table serves multiple business domains. You can also have semantic models that connect across multiple dbt models through entity relationships.</p>
<p>The only hard constraint is that every semantic model must reference an existing dbt model as its base. Semantic models are an annotation layer; they add meaning to structure that already exists.</p>
<h3>Aligning With Your Data Catalogue</h3>
<p>If your organisation already maintains metric definitions in a data catalogue (Alation, Atlan, DataHub, or similar), the semantic layer should <strong>implement</strong> those definitions, not replace them. The catalogue defines what a metric means in business terms. The semantic layer defines how that metric is computed in code.</p>
<p>The goal is alignment: for every metric definition in your catalogue, there’s a corresponding YAML definition in your dbt project that makes it executable and enforceable.</p>
<h3>Testing and Validation</h3>
<p>Metrics should be tested like any other code. Use MetricFlow commands to query metrics locally during development and validate results against known baselines. Add dbt tests to the underlying models for data quality constraints. And during rollout, compare outputs to existing “trusted” reports — if they don’t match, you’ve surfaced a bug in either the report or the definition, and both outcomes are valuable.</p>
<h3>Multi-Platform Environments</h3>
<p>If your organisation runs multiple data platforms — a warehouse for reporting and a lakehouse for advanced analytics, for example — the semantic layer can serve both. MetricFlow generates platform-specific SQL, so the same YAML definitions produce correct queries whether they’re executed on Redshift, Databricks, Snowflake, BigQuery, Postgres, or Trino.</p>
<p>This is especially valuable for organisations with hybrid architectures. Metric consistency across platforms without maintaining parallel definitions.</p>
<h3>The Compounding Return</h3>
<p>Here’s why this investment pays off disproportionately over time:</p>
<p><strong>Day one</strong>, you get metric consistency across your core reports. One definition, one source of truth. That alone eliminates a significant class of “which number is right?” issues.</p>
<p><strong>Month three</strong>, your BI tools are querying the semantic layer natively. Self-serve analytics starts to become real because business users can trust the numbers without manual validation.</p>
<p><strong>Month six</strong>, you’ve built a library of composable metrics. New metric requests that used to take days of SQL development now take hours of YAML composition from existing, tested building blocks.</p>
<p><strong>Month twelve</strong>, an AI agent connects to your semantic layer through the Model Context Protocol. It answers natural language questions using the same governed metrics as your dashboards. You didn’t build anything new for AI — the foundation you built for reporting consistency was already exactly what AI needed.</p>
<p>The governed foundation you build today becomes the structured context that every future consumer — human or machine — inherits.</p>
<p><img src="https://medium.com/_/stat?event=post.clientViewed&referrerSource=full_rss&postId=93d7e29e6ab3" alt=""></p>

1d:T27b7,<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*lG921DnbZtP4H3oL4CQfuw.png" alt=""></p>
<h3>The Problem: Metric Disagreement Across Tools and Teams</h3>
<p>Most data teams have encountered this situation: two reports that should show the same number don’t. Finance reports one revenue figure. Marketing reports another. An analyst pulls a third from the warehouse directly. The definitions look similar, but the filters, time grains, or inclusion criteria differ just enough to produce different results.</p>
<p>This is a common and well-documented problem in enterprise data. It’s not caused by bad data or incompetent teams — it’s caused by an architectural gap. In most modern data stacks, <strong>there is no single, governed layer where business metrics are defined and enforced</strong>. Instead, metric definitions are scattered across BI tools, spreadsheets, SQL scripts, and ad-hoc notebooks, each maintained independently with no mechanism for consistency.</p>
<p>The practical consequences are significant. Stakeholders spend time reconciling numbers rather than acting on them. Data teams get pulled into validation work that crowds out higher-value analysis. And trust in data erodes — once a number is wrong in a high-visibility context, rebuilding confidence takes considerably longer than fixing the definition.</p>
<h3>Where Metric Definitions Actually Live</h3>
<p>In most organisations, business logic is embedded in several places simultaneously:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>BI tool calculated fields</strong> — metric definitions inside Tableau, Power BI, Looker, or similar platforms, scoped to that tool only</li>
<li><strong>Spreadsheets</strong> — Excel or Google Sheets workbooks with formulas that encode business rules, often undocumented and unversioned</li>
<li><strong>Ad-hoc SQL</strong> — queries saved locally by analysts, sometimes shared via Slack or email, rarely maintained after initial use</li>
<li><strong>LookML/PowerBI/Tableau/semantic definitions within BI platforms</strong> — governed within a single tool but not portable to other consumers</li>
<li><strong>dbt model logic</strong> — business rules embedded in SQL transformations, but not formalised as reusable metric definitions</li>
</ul>
<p>Each of these represents a reasonable attempt to capture business logic. The problem isn’t any individual approach — it’s the duplication. When the same metric is independently defined in five places, there are five opportunities for drift. And drift is the default state; consistency requires active governance that most stacks don’t provide.</p>
<p>This pattern – critical business logic existing outside any formal governance framework – is sometimes called <strong>&#39;Shadow BI&#39;.</strong> It’s not a failure of discipline. It’s a predictable outcome of architectures that don’t provide a dedicated layer for metric governance.</p>
<h3>Why the BI Layer Is the Wrong Place for Metric Governance</h3>
<p>The most common location for metric definitions is the BI layer — the tools with which business users consume data. This is intuitive: that’s where metrics are used, so that’s where they get defined.</p>
<p>But the BI layer has structural limitations that make it unsuitable as the system of record for business metrics.</p>
<p><strong>Tool specificity.</strong> A metric defined in Tableau doesn’t exist in Power BI, in a Python notebook, or in an API endpoint. If your organisation uses multiple consumption tools — or if non-BI consumers need access to the same metric — you’re maintaining parallel definitions with no synchronisation mechanism.</p>
<p><strong>Limited version control.</strong> Most BI tools don’t provide Git-style version history, peer review workflows, or rollback capabilities for metric definitions. A definition change is typically a UI action with no formal review process. In contrast, a metric defined as &#39;code&#39; in a dbt project goes through a pull request, is reviewed by peers, and has a full audit trail.</p>
<p><strong>No extensibility to non-BI consumers.</strong> When an AI agent, a machine learning pipeline, a reverse ETL process, or an embedded analytics product needs a metric, it can’t query the BI tool’s internal semantic model. The definition must be re-implemented — introducing another copy, another opportunity for drift.</p>
<h3>The Analyst Bottleneck</h3>
<p>Without a governed metric layer, the person who “knows the data” becomes the de facto source of truth. They are the ones who know that “active users” means “distinct users who logged in within 30 days, excluding internal accounts and test users, at a monthly grain.”</p>
<p>This creates a well-known bottleneck pattern. Self-serve analytics doesn’t function in practice because every answer requires validation by someone with institutional knowledge. That person’s capacity for strategic analytical work — the work they were hired for — decreases as the organisation’s data footprint grows.</p>
<p>This is a scaling problem. It gets worse, not better, as more teams, tools, and consumers enter the picture.</p>
<h3>The Compounding Effect</h3>
<p>Every new consumer of data — a new dashboard, a new analytics platform, a new AI-powered feature — inherits the current state of metric inconsistency. Each new consumer is another place where business logic gets independently defined.</p>
<p>This compounding is why metric drift accelerates over time. Organisations don’t converge towards consistency by default; they diverge. The cost of remediation increases with each new silo, which is why addressing the architectural gap early yields disproportionate long-term value.</p>
<h3>The Architectural Fix: A Semantic Layer in the Transformation Layer</h3>
<p>The solution is conceptually straightforward: <strong>move metric definitions out of the BI layer and into the transformation layer</strong> — the governed, version-controlled, tested layer where data models already live.</p>
<p>This is what a semantic layer provides. It’s an intermediary between your data models and your consumption tools. Instead of each tool defining metrics independently, every consumer — dashboards, notebooks, AI agents, and APIs — queries metrics through a single governed layer.</p>
<p><strong>Without a semantic layer:</strong></p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*-fWWDojig4Sn6d6-raEH5Q.png" alt=""></p>
<p>without governed semantic layer, metrics are scattered — no single source of truth</p>
<p><strong>With a semantic layer:</strong></p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*fgrAILPlHAkKf0RYvaGCxw.png" alt=""></p>
<p>The semantic layer doesn’t replace existing tools or require warehouse restructuring. It adds a governed definition layer that every downstream consumer inherits.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*7vLwLdi8SS4wNAxqHitLEA.png" alt=""></p>
<h3>Metrics as Code</h3>
<p>The modern approach to semantic layers — particularly the dbt Semantic Layer powered by MetricFlow — treats metric definitions as code. Definitions are written in YAML files that live alongside dbt models in a Git repository. They go through pull requests, are tested in CI/CD, and carry clear ownership.</p>
<p>This applies the same governance model that software engineering has used for decades — version control, peer review, automated testing, and audit trails — to business logic. It’s a well-understood pattern; the only new aspect is applying it to metric definitions rather than application code.</p>
<p>MetricFlow, the engine that powers the dbt Semantic Layer, is now open source under the Apache 2.0 licence and is part of the <strong>Open Semantic Interchange (OSI)</strong> initiative — a cross-vendor effort toward portable semantic standards involving dbt Labs, Snowflake, Salesforce, Atlan, Alation, and others.</p>
<h3>The Compounding Value of Governed Data Definitions</h3>
<p>The metric consistency problem is ultimately a governance gap. The data exists. The tools exist. What’s missing is an architectural layer that ensures every consumer — every tool, every team, every automated system — works from the same definitions.</p>
<p>Building that layer is an investment that compounds. Every new dashboard, every new AI workload, every new data consumer inherits the governance you’ve built. The alternative — allowing metric definitions to drift across ungoverned silos — also compounds, but in the wrong direction.</p>
<p>The tools and standards for governed semantic layers have matured significantly over the past two years, with MetricFlow’s open-sourcing and the OSI initiative signalling broader industry convergence. For data teams evaluating where to invest, this is a foundation worth building early.</p>

1e:T19e0,<h3>dbt fusion : Under the Hood—The Technical Architecture</h3>
<h4><em><strong>More Than Just “Faster”</strong></em></h4>
<p>The dbt Fusion Engine represents a fundamental architectural evolution from the legacy Python-based dbt Core runtime, designed to deliver high performance, developer productivity, and advanced governance.</p>
<p>When developers first hear “30x faster parsing,” they often think it’s just about optimised code. But fusion’s speed is a byproduct of something more profound: Fusion truly understands your SQL code and has a full view of what it means and how it propagates across your entire data lineage.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/753/1*hTWf2vUUa-46kp1V9yXj0A.png" alt=""></p>
<p>Diagram created by author using mermaid</p>
<h3>Foundation: The Rust Runtime (dbt-fusion)</h3>
<p>The primary architectural shift is the complete rewrite of the core parsing and compilation logic in <strong>Rust</strong>. This decision addresses the performance and concurrency limitations of the Python runtime.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Performance:</strong> Rust compiles to a single, native binary with zero runtime overhead, enabling the <strong>30x faster project parsing</strong> widely cited in dbt Labs documentation. This is crucial for maintaining developer flow in large-scale projects.</li>
<li><strong>Correctness and Safety:</strong> Rust’s memory safety and concurrency primitives enhance the reliability and stability of the engine, particularly in long-running IDE sessions.</li>
<li><strong>Deployment:</strong> The engine is distributed as a standalone binary, requiring no external Python dependencies beyond the necessary database drivers</li>
</ul>
<h3>Compilation and Semantic Validation: SQL Comprehension</h3>
<p>The Fusion Engine moves from treating SQL as a text template (Jinja) to a language that can be semantically understood.</p>
<p>The engine performs <strong>Level 2 SQL Comprehension</strong> using a <strong>Logical Plan</strong> (Intermediate Representation) built via Apache DataFusion. This allows for “Shift Left” validation:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Zero-Warehouse Validation:</strong> Unlike dbt Core, which must send a query to the warehouse to see if it fails, Fusion builds a local <strong>semantic graph.</strong> It knows the schema of your upstream models from previous runs (via the manifest) and the AST of your current model.</li>
<li><strong>Column-Level Tracking:</strong> If you delete a column in stg_customers, the Semantic Validator immediately flags every downstream model referencing that column with a &quot;Column Not Found&quot; error in the IDE—<strong>before you ever hit &quot;Run.&quot;</strong></li>
<li><strong>Type Safety:</strong> It validates that you aren’t trying to perform a mathematical operation on a string, catching type mismatches instantly.</li>
</ul>
<h3>Developer Interface: Language Server Protocol (LSP)</h3>
<p>The LSP is the standard <strong>decoupling mechanism</strong> that translates the Fusion Engine’s complex contextual understanding into real-time interactive features within IDEs (dbt Studio, VS Code Extension).</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Real-time Intelligence:</strong> The engine hosts a <strong>dbt Language Server</strong> that communicates via the LSP to provide features like IntelliSense, autocompletion for models and columns, Go-to-defintion, and inline error highlighting.</li>
<li><strong>Incremental Compilation:</strong> The LSP enables the engine to track and recompile only the delta of changes, ensuring a near-instantaneous feedback loop for the developer.</li>
</ul>
<h3>Data I/O: Arrow Database Connectivity (ADBC)</h3>
<p>For high-performance interaction with the data warehouse, Fusion leverages the <strong>Apache Arrow</strong> ecosystem.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Columnar Data Transfer:</strong> ADBC drivers facilitate fast, memory-efficient data transfer using the Arrow columnar format, optimizing data movement during tasks like query previews and data quality checks.</li>
<li><strong>Unified Interface:</strong> ADBC provides a standardized interface for adapters, reducing complexity and increasing speed compared to traditional row-based ODBC/JDBC connectors.</li>
</ul>
<h3>Execution Logic: State-Aware Orchestration</h3>
<p>In a dbt Cloud context, the Fusion Engine informs <strong>dbt Orchestrator</strong> components.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Model State Tracking:</strong> By tracking the production manifest and comparing it against source data freshness and code changes, the engine enables state-aware runs.</li>
<li><strong>Cost Optimization:</strong> This orchestration logic allows jobs to automatically determine the minimal set of models that <em>need</em> to be rebuilt, leading to significant <strong>warehouse compute cost reductions.</strong></li>
</ul>
<p>Fusion isn’t just optimized Python — it’s a fundamental architectural reimagining built on modern languages (Rust), industry standards (LSP, ADBC), and advanced SQL compilation. This foundation enables experiences impossible in dbt Core.</p>

This feature helps teams create more intuitive and visually organized data lineage graphs by color-coding models based on their purpose, domain, or layer in the data architecture.</p>

20:T28ea,<p><em><strong>Real code, real patterns, real results</strong></em></p>
<p>You understand the concept of data contracts. You’re convinced that shift left governance is the right direction. Now comes the practical question: “How do I actually implement this?”</p>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://medium.com/@aradsouza/data-contracts-the-missing-link-in-your-data-engineering-strategy-7dbc1e95e208">Data Contracts: The Missing Link in Your Data Engineering Strategy</a></p>
</blockquote>
<blockquote>
<p><a href="https://medium.com/@aradsouza/shift-left-governance-why-you-are-fixing-data-problems-at-the-wrong-time-dbaf7895520d">Shift Left Governance: Why You Are Fixing Data Problems at the Wrong Time</a></p>
</blockquote>
<p>If your data transformation layer uses dbt — and increasingly, it probably does — you’re in luck. dbt has native support for data contracts that makes enforcement straightforward and integrates seamlessly with your existing workflows.</p>
<p>This article walks through the technical implementation, from basic concepts to advanced patterns, with real examples you can adapt for your own projects.</p>
<h3>Why dbt for Data Contracts?</h3>
<p>dbt (data build tool) has become the standard for analytics engineering because it brings software engineering practices to data transformation. Version control, testing, documentation, dependency management — everything that makes code maintainable now applies to data.</p>
<p>Data contracts are a <strong>natural extension</strong>. Instead of transforming data and hoping it meets expectations, you explicitly define those expectations and validate them with every run.</p>
<p>The beauty of dbt’s implementation is that <strong>contracts aren’t separate from your data models</strong> — they’re integrated directly into the model configurations where they belong. The same YAML files that define your models now also define the contracts those models must satisfy.</p>
<h3>The Anatomy of a dbt Data Contract</h3>
<p>A dbt data contract has several components:</p>
<p><strong>Schema definition</strong> that specifies columns, data types, and constraints. This is the foundation — the structural agreement about what fields exist and what types they should be.</p>
<p><strong>Contract enforcement</strong> that validates actual data against the contract specification during every dbt run. When enabled, dbt checks that the data produced by your model matches the contract before making it available downstream.</p>
<p><strong>Data quality tests</strong> that go beyond schema validation to enforce business logic. While contracts handle structure, tests handle semantics — the business rules that make data meaningful.</p>
<p><strong>Versioning</strong> that manages contract changes over time. When you need to evolve a contract, dbt’s versioning features let you maintain backward compatibility while introducing new versions.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*-IGsbUDR6aoQs6rPc35qYA.png" alt=""></p>
<p>Pillars of data contract (Image by author)</p>
<p>Let’s look at how these pieces fit together in practice.</p>
<h4>Basic Contract Implementation</h4>
<p>Here’s a customer model with an enforced contract:</p>
<pre><code># models/customers.yml
version: 2
models:
 - name: customers
 config:
 contract:
 enforced: true
 
 columns:
 - name: customer_id
 data_type: string
 constraints:
 - type: not_null
 - type: unique
 
 - name: email
 data_type: string
 constraints:
 - type: not_null
 
 - name: created_at
 data_type: timestamp
 constraints:
 - type: not_null
</code></pre><p><strong>The key:</strong> <code>contract: enforced: true</code></p>
<p>This tells dbt to validate the contract on every run. Wrong column names, incorrect types, or missing fields? <strong>The model fails before data gets written.</strong></p>
<h4>Adding Business Logic with Tests</h4>
<p>Contracts enforce structure. Tests enforce business rules:</p>
<pre><code>columns:
 - name: order_amount
 data_type: decimal
 constraints:
 - type: not_null
 tests:
 # Revenue must be non-negative
 - dbt_utils.expression_is_true:
 expression: &quot;&gt;= 0&quot;
 
 - name: order_timestamp
 tests:
 # Orders within last 2 years
 - dbt_utils.expression_is_true:
 expression: &quot;&gt;= current_timestamp - interval &#x27;2 years&#x27;&quot;
 
 - name: customer_id
 tests:
 # Referential integrity
 - relationships:
 to: ref(&#x27;customers&#x27;)
 field: customer_id
</code></pre><p><strong>Contracts + Tests = Comprehensive Validation</strong></p>
<p>Structure correctness + content validity = trusted data.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*I6lC-R3Vt21YcSqrauuAWw.png" alt=""></p>
<h3>Versioning: Managing Contract Evolution</h3>
<p>Here’s a reality of data systems: requirements change. You need to add new fields, deprecate old ones, or modify business logic. How do you evolve contracts without breaking everything downstream?</p>
<p>dbt’s versioning feature provides the answer. You can maintain multiple versions of a model simultaneously, giving consumers time to migrate.</p>
<p><strong>dbt’s versioning feature:</strong></p>
<pre><code>models:
 - name: customers
 latest_version: 2
 
 versions:
 - v: 1
 columns:
 - name: customer_id
 - name: email
 # v1: basic fields only
 
 - v: 2
 columns:
 - name: customer_id
 - name: email
 - name: phone_number
 - name: preferred_contact_method
 # v2: adds contact preferences
</code></pre><p>Now consumers can reference either <code>{{ ref(&#39;customers&#39;, v=1) }}</code> or <code>{{ ref(&#39;customers&#39;, v=2) }}</code>. You maintain both versions while teams migrate, then deprecate v1 once adoption of v2 is complete.</p>
<p>This pattern enables backwards-compatible changes. New consumers get the enhanced version. Existing consumers continue working unchanged. Migration happens at a controlled pace without a sudden breakage.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*zaVwGwcNwQuv4-r6-Lt78g.png" alt=""></p>
<h3>Integrating Contracts into CI/CD</h3>
<p>The real power of data contracts emerges when you integrate them into your continuous integration/continuous deployment pipeline. Every code change gets validated before merge, catching contract violations in development rather than production.</p>
<p>A typical CI workflow looks like this:</p>
<pre><code># .github/workflows/dbt_ci.yml
name: dbt CI
on:
 pull_request:
 branches: [main]
jobs:
 test:
 runs-on: ubuntu-latest
 steps:
 - uses: actions/checkout@v2
 
 - name: Install dbt
 run: pip install dbt-databricks
 
 - name: dbt deps
 run: dbt deps
 
 - name: dbt compile
 run: dbt compile
 # Validates that SQL is valid
 
 - name: dbt run (contract check)
 run: dbt run --select state:modified+
 # Builds modified models, enforcing contracts
 
 - name: dbt test
 run: dbt test --select state:modified+
 # Runs data quality tests
</code></pre><p>When a developer opens a pull request that modifies a model, this workflow runs automatically. If their changes violate a contract — maybe they removed a column that downstream models depend on, or changed a data type incompatibly — the build fails with a clear error message.</p>
<p>The developer sees the issue immediately, while the full context is still fresh. They can adjust their changes, coordinate with downstream consumers, or create a new contract version as appropriate.</p>
<p>Compare this to discovering the problem in production after deployment. Instead of a four-hour investigation and emergency rollback, you get a two-minute fix in development.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*pi-e1FvrqoDQc2NFFCGJnA.png" alt=""></p>
<h3>Advanced Pattern: Cross-Database Contracts</h3>
<p>In modern data architectures, you often need to share data across different platforms or business units. Data contracts become even more critical here, ensuring that data maintains quality and structure as it crosses boundaries.</p>
<p>At each boundary, contracts enforce quality:</p>
<pre><code># Foundational data product contract
models:
 - name: fct_customer_interactions
 description: &quot;Foundation fact table for all customer interactions&quot;
 config:
 contract:
 enforced: true
 columns:
 - name: interaction_id
 data_type: string
 constraints:
 - type: not_null
 - type: unique
 
 - name: customer_id
 data_type: string
 constraints:
 - type: not_null
 
 - name: interaction_timestamp
 data_type: timestamp
 constraints:
 - type: not_null
 
 - name: interaction_type
 data_type: string
 constraints:
 - type: not_null
 
 - name: channel
 data_type: string
 constraints:
 - type: not_null
</code></pre><p>Consumers in marketing, finance, or other domains can confidently build derived products on this foundation, knowing the contract guarantees specific structure and quality.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*2RTU5_FJCfM2-A-dPOcdTg.png" alt=""></p>
<h3>Contract Documentation: The Human Side</h3>
<p>While contracts enforce technical agreements, documentation makes those agreements understandable. dbt automatically generates documentation from your contract specifications, creating a browsable catalog of all data products and their guarantees.</p>
<p>Every contract should include:</p>
<p><strong>Clear descriptions</strong> that explain business context, not just technical details. “Customer email address for marketing communications” is better than just “email field.”</p>
<p><strong>Ownership information</strong> that identifies who maintains the contract and who to contact for questions or changes.</p>
<p><strong>SLA commitments</strong> that specify freshness expectations, update schedules, and reliability guarantees.</p>
<p><strong>Change history</strong> that documents contract evolution over time, helping consumers understand what changed and why.</p>
<p>This documentation becomes your team’s single source of truth. New team members onboarding can browse the contract catalog to understand available data products. Analysts can self-serve without endless Slack questions. Governance teams can audit compliance without manual spreadsheets.</p>

21:T1aec,<p><em><strong>Show your CTO the numbers that justify the investment</strong></em></p>
<h3>The Question Every Leader Asks</h3>
<p>“Why invest engineering time in data contracts when we have features to ship?”</p>
<p>Fair question. But here’s the hidden truth in your incident logs: <strong>You’re already paying for data quality — reactively, expensively, unpredictably.</strong></p>
<p>Data contracts shift costs from reactive firefighting to proactive prevention. The ROI is measurable, substantial, and fast.</p>
<h3>The Hidden Costs You’re Already Paying</h3>
<p>Pull your incident logs. Count data quality issues from last quarter:</p>
<ul>
<li>Pipeline failures</li>
<li>Schema changes breaking systems</li>
<li>Missing/incorrect data in reports</li>
<li>Compliance incidents</li>
</ul>
<p>Calculate the engineering hours spent investigating and resolving each.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*1x243UA3-ytcODaVCYW2xg.png" alt=""></p>
<h4>Typical Pattern Without Contracts</h4>
<p><strong>Incidents:</strong> 15–20 critical issues/month<br> <strong>Resolution:</strong> 4–6 hours average per incident<br> <strong>Engineers:</strong> 2–3 involved in most investigations<br> <strong>Total:</strong> 120–360 engineering hours/month on firefighting</p>
<p><strong>At $150/hour blended rate:</strong> $18K-$54K monthly<br> <strong>Annual cost:</strong> $216K-$648K in incident response alone</p>
<p>And that’s just <strong>direct costs</strong>. Add:</p>
<ul>
<li>Stakeholder validation time (analysts manually checking data)</li>
<li>Delayed decisions (waiting for “real numbers”)</li>
<li>Opportunity cost (features not built while firefighting)</li>
</ul>
<h3>The ROI of Data Contracts: Real Numbers</h3>
<p>Now let’s look at what changes when you implement data contracts systematically.</p>
<p><strong>70% Reduction in Data Incidents</strong></p>
<p>Organizations with mature data contract implementations report dramatic drops in production issues. The mechanism is straightforward: proactive validation at the source prevents most issues from ever entering your systems.</p>
<p>If you’re currently handling 15–20 critical incidents per month, that drops to 2–3. Your monthly incident response time falls from 120–360 hours to 15–45 hours. Annual savings: $160,000-$480,000 in direct engineering costs.</p>
<p>But the savings compound beyond direct hours. Fewer incidents mean:</p>
<ul>
<li>Reduced on-call burden (happier engineers, lower turnover)</li>
<li>Less context switching (higher productivity on planned work)</li>
<li>Fewer emergency escalations (preserved stakeholder relationships)</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>60% Faster Issue Resolution</strong></p>
<p>When issues do occur with data contracts in place, they’re caught faster and resolved quickly. Instead of discovering problems days later through stakeholder complaints, contract violations are detected immediately when code is deployed.</p>
<p>Mean time to detection (MTTD) drops from days to seconds. Mean time to resolution (MTTR) falls from 4–6 hours to 15–30 minutes. The investigation that used to require tracing through layers of transformations now points directly to the contract violation with clear error messages.</p>
<p>This speed improvement has cascading benefits:</p>
<ul>
<li>Problems are fixed while the context is fresh (easier debugging)</li>
<li>Bad data doesn’t propagate through multiple systems (contained blast radius)</li>
<li>Stakeholders maintain trust (proactive communication, quick fixes)</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>40% Less Engineering Overhead</strong></p>
<p>When you’re not spending 60–80% of your time on data quality firefighting, engineering bandwidth opens up for value creation. Teams report shifting from 70/30 reactive/proactive work to 20/80 or better.</p>
<p>What does your team do with that reclaimed time?</p>
<ul>
<li>Build new data products that drive business value</li>
<li>Implement advanced analytics capabilities</li>
<li>Improve platform performance and scalability</li>
<li>Reduce technical debt</li>
<li>Innovate on ML/AI applications</li>
</ul>
<p>The opportunity value of this bandwidth often exceeds the direct cost savings from reduced incidents.</p>
<p><strong>85% Improved Data Trust</strong></p>
<p>Perhaps the most valuable but hardest to quantify: stakeholder confidence in data increases dramatically. When consumers know that data has been validated at the source with automated contracts, they stop second-guessing every number.</p>
<p>This trust enables:</p>
<ul>
<li>Self-service analytics (users don’t need hand-holding)</li>
<li>Faster decision-making (no delays waiting for “validated” numbers)</li>
<li>Reduced analytics team burden (fewer ad-hoc validation requests)</li>
<li>Broader data democratisation (more users can safely access data)</li>
</ul>
<p>Organisations measure this through Net Promoter Score (NPS) surveys of data consumers, tracking escalations and complaints, and monitoring self-service adoption rates.</p>
<h3>Additional ROI Drivers</h3>
<h4>Accelerated Onboarding</h4>
<p>50% faster startup for new analytics use cases. Read the contract, understand guarantees, start using — no trial and error learning quirks.</p>
<h4>Reduced Compliance Risk</h4>
<p>Automated governance prevents violations. Lower probability of regulatory fines (single GDPR fine = millions of euros).</p>
<h4>Faster Time-to-Insight</h4>
<p>An analyst who spent 2 days validating now concludes in 2 hours. Multiply across the analytics organisation.</p>
<h4>Improved Collaboration</h4>
<p>Clear contracts = clear interfaces between teams. Less friction, miscommunication, and finger-pointing.</p>
<h3>Addressing Common Objections</h3>
<h4>“We don’t have time with the current workload”</h4>
<p><strong>Response:</strong> You’re already spending the time — reactively. Contracts shift to proactive prevention (more efficient). Investment pays back in 3–6 months.</p>
<h4>“Our data is too complex for simple contracts”</h4>
<p><strong>Response:</strong> Start simple with basic schema validation. Each contract delivers value. Sophistication comes later.</p>
<h4>“We’ll lose velocity maintaining contracts”</h4>
<p><strong>Response:</strong> Contracts are evergreen, need minimal maintenance. Velocity gain from reduced firefighting far exceeds overhead.</p>
<h4>“Producers won’t adopt this”</h4>
<p><strong>Response:</strong> Show value. When engineers see contracts that prevent production breaks, they appreciate the safety net. Start with willing teams.</p>

22:T1acf,<blockquote>
<p><em>Turning 60–80% firefighting time into proactive innovation</em></p>
