This article was originally published at https://medium.com/@aradsouza/from-4-days-to-2-hours-how-context-aware-ai-transformed-our-code-reviews-f30b5da9db6b
</code></pre>28:T3252,<p>Read the main article in the series before doing a deep dive.</p>
<h4><a href="https://medium.com/@aradsouza/from-4-days-to-2-hours-how-context-aware-ai-transformed-our-code-reviews-f30b5da9db6b">From 4 Days to 2 Hours: How Context-Aware AI Transformed Our Code Reviews</a></h4>
<h3>Part 1: How We Got Here and Why Standard Solutions Aren’t Working</h3>
<p>Let me start with something that might sound contradictory: despite having more automation, better tooling, and smarter CI/CD pipelines than ever before, code reviews have become slower and more painful.</p>
<p>If you’re a developer, you know the frustration. You submit a PR that you’re confident about. The code works. Tests pass. You’ve followed the patterns you’ve seen elsewhere in the codebase. Then you wait. And wait. Two days later, you get feedback about missing a specific configuration, or not following a pattern that’s documented somewhere in a wiki you’ve never been able to find.</p>
<p>If you’re a reviewer, you know the exhaustion. You’re checking the same things on every PR — is the config correct? Are the tests comprehensive? Is the documentation updated? Did they follow the twelve different standards we’ve accumulated over the years? Your brain hurts, you miss things, and you’re acutely aware that you’ve become the bottleneck holding everyone back.</p>
<p>This is the reality for most engineering teams working with data pipelines, infrastructure as code, or any domain with established patterns and standards. And it’s getting worse, not better.</p>
<h3>The numbers that made us realize something was broken</h3>
<p>Before I explain what we tried (and what eventually worked), here’s where we started:</p>
<ul>
<li>Pull requests taking <strong>2–4 days</strong> on average from submission to approval</li>
<li><strong>35% of commits</strong> failing pre-commit hooks and CI checks</li>
<li><strong>2–4 revision cycles</strong> per PR on average before approval</li>
<li>Test coverage hovering around <strong>60%</strong></li>
<li>Documentation compliance at <strong>45%</strong></li>
<li>Code standards adherence at <strong>70%</strong> (meaning 30% of merged code didn’t fully follow our patterns)</li>
</ul>
<p>The cost wasn’t just in time. Developer morale was suffering. “Why does it take so long to get anything merged?” became a constant refrain. Reviewers were burning out. “I spend all day reviewing code and never get to my actual work” was equally common.</p>
<p>We had documentation. We had standards. We had wiki pages explaining exactly how things should be done. None of it mattered because documentation doesn’t review code — people do. And people are inconsistent, they get tired, and they can’t possibly keep every project-specific rule in their heads while reviewing dozens of PRs.</p>
<h3>Why this isn’t just about being “too slow”</h3>
<p>The deeper problem isn’t the time itself — it’s what that time represents. Slow code reviews are a symptom of several underlying issues:</p>
<p><strong>Knowledge is trapped in people’s heads.</strong> Every experienced engineer on your team knows dozens of patterns, gotchas, and standards that never make it into documentation. Or if they do, the documentation is so scattered and outdated that nobody reads it.</p>
<p><strong>Reviewers become gatekeepers, not collaborators.</strong> When the review process is primarily about catching compliance issues, the reviewer’s role devolves into being a human checklist. That’s frustrating for everyone and wastes the reviewer’s actual expertise.</p>
<p><strong>Standards drift over time.</strong> Different reviewers emphasize different things. What passes with one reviewer gets rejected by another. New team members pick up inconsistent patterns depending on who reviews their code.</p>
<p><strong>The learning loop is broken.</strong> When feedback comes days after you wrote the code, you’ve already context-switched to something else. The learning doesn’t stick the way it would with immediate feedback.</p>
<p><strong>Junior developers struggle disproportionately.</strong> They don’t know what they don’t know. They see code that works, submit it, and get surprised by feedback about standards they’d never heard of. The onboarding curve is brutal.</p>
<h3>The first attempt: “just use AI”</h3>
<p>Like many teams, when AI coding assistants became available, we were optimistic. Finally, a tool that could help with code reviews! We enabled GitHub Copilot for the team and waited for the magic to happen.</p>
<p>It didn’t.</p>
<p>Don’t get me wrong — Copilot is impressive. It can autocomplete code, suggest refactorings, and catch common issues. But when we asked it to review our data transformation code, it gave us feedback like:</p>
<ul>
<li>“This SELECT statement could be written more clearly”</li>
<li>“Consider adding comments to explain complex logic”</li>
<li>“This function could be broken into smaller functions”</li>
</ul>
<p>Helpful? Sure, in a generic sense. But it completely missed the things that actually mattered to us:</p>
<ul>
<li>It didn’t know that certain models required specific pre-processing steps</li>
<li>It didn’t catch missing configurations that were mandatory in our architecture</li>
<li>It had no idea about our naming conventions or organizational patterns</li>
<li>It couldn’t enforce our testing requirements or documentation standards</li>
</ul>
<p>The subject matter expert still had to do the entire manual review. We’d just added another tool without actually solving the problem.</p>
<h3>Understanding why generic AI falls short</h3>
<p>Here’s the fundamental issue: AI coding assistants are trained on millions of repositories. They understand general software engineering principles, common patterns, and language-specific best practices. What they don’t have — what they can’t have out of the box — is knowledge of your specific architecture, your team’s accumulated decisions, and the context of why you do things a particular way.</p>
<p>Think about what a senior engineer knows about your codebase:</p>
<ul>
<li>Which models are part of which domains</li>
<li>Why certain patterns exist (often learned from painful incidents)</li>
<li>What the unwritten rules are that everyone just “knows”</li>
<li>How different parts of the system interact</li>
<li>What the common mistakes are and how to avoid them</li>
</ul>
<p>Generic AI has none of this context. It’s like hiring a brilliant engineer who’s an expert in the programming language but knows nothing about your business, your architecture, or your accumulated wisdom.</p>
<h3>The manual review trap</h3>
<p>So we were stuck with manual reviews, which created their own set of problems:</p>
<p><strong>Inconsistency.</strong> Reviewer A might care deeply about test coverage. Reviewer B might focus on documentation. Reviewer C might be strict about configuration patterns. The feedback you get depends on who reviews your code.</p>
<p><strong>Fatigue.</strong> Checking the same twenty things on every PR is mind-numbing work. You start skipping checks, or your eyes glaze over, or you rubber-stamp things just to get through the queue.</p>
<p><strong>Bottlenecks.</strong> If you have one or two senior engineers who really know the standards, they become mandatory reviewers on everything. They’re overwhelmed, PRs pile up, and nothing moves.</p>
<p><strong>Knowledge silos.</strong> When feedback is given through PR comments, it helps that one developer on that one PR. The next developer makes the same mistake because they never saw that feedback.</p>
<p><strong>Defensive development.</strong> Developers start to fear code reviews. They hold back on innovation because “the reviewer won’t like it.” They submit smaller, safer PRs that don’t really improve the codebase.</p>
<h3>What we needed but didn’t have</h3>
<p>Looking back, what we really needed was:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Instant feedback</strong> that happens while writing code or immediately upon PR submission</li>
<li><strong>Consistent enforcement</strong> that doesn’t vary based on who’s reviewing or how tired they are</li>
<li><strong>Context-aware suggestions</strong> that understand our specific architecture and standards</li>
<li><strong>Teaching, not just catching</strong> so developers learn patterns instead of just fixing individual issues</li>
<li><strong>Reviewer augmentation</strong> that handles the routine checks so humans can focus on strategic feedback</li>
</ol>
<p>Standard tooling gave us some of these. CI/CD catches issues, but only after you’ve committed and pushed. Linters enforce code style, but not architectural patterns. Documentation exists, but nobody reads all of it consistently.</p>
<p>What we needed was a way to take the knowledge in senior engineers’ heads and make it instantly accessible to everyone, all the time, right at the moment they need it.</p>
<h3>The stakes are higher than you think</h3>
<p>Before we move on to solutions, it’s worth understanding why this matters beyond just “code reviews are annoying.”</p>
<p><strong>Time-to-market suffers.</strong> When every feature requires multiple days of review cycles, your velocity drops significantly. What should take a week takes two or three.</p>
<p><strong>Innovation slows.</strong> Developers become conservative. They stick with patterns they know will pass review rather than trying better approaches that might get questioned.</p>
<p><strong>Knowledge doesn’t transfer.</strong> New team members learn slowly because the feedback loop is so long. What could be learned in weeks takes months.</p>
<p><strong>Technical debt accumulates.</strong> When standards aren’t enforced consistently, the codebase gradually drifts. Different parts follow different patterns. Inconsistency becomes the norm.</p>
<p><strong>People leave.</strong> Both developers (frustrated by slow feedback) and reviewers (exhausted by the burden) become dissatisfied. Your best people find opportunities elsewhere.</p>
<p>The cost of slow, inconsistent code reviews is enormous — it just shows up gradually rather than all at once, so it’s easy to accept as “just how things are.”</p>
<h3>What makes code reviews slow isn’t what you think</h3>
<p>Most teams try to speed up code reviews by optimizing the wrong things:</p>
<ul>
<li>Adding more reviewers (which just spreads the bottleneck across more people)</li>
<li>Creating review checklists (which nobody uses consistently)</li>
<li>Setting SLAs for review time (which creates pressure but not capability)</li>
<li>Simplifying standards (which just lowers quality)</li>
</ul>
<p>None of these address the core problem: the knowledge required to do a good review is expensive to access. It’s in people’s heads, in scattered documentation, in historical context that takes years to accumulate.</p>
<p>The breakthrough comes from making that knowledge cheap to access — instant, consistent, and embedded right in the development workflow.</p>
<h3>The path forward</h3>
<p>So that’s where we were: slow reviews, frustrated developers, exhausted reviewers, inconsistent standards, and none of the obvious solutions working.</p>
<p>What eventually worked wasn’t a new tool or a process change. It was finding a way to teach AI our specific context — to take the generic capabilities of AI coding assistants and specialize them for our exact environment, patterns, and standards.</p>
<p>In the next part, we’ll dive into exactly how we did that, what changed, and why context-aware AI is fundamentally different from generic AI for code reviews.</p>
<p>But the key insight is this: the problem isn’t the lack of intelligence (human or artificial). It’s the lack of accessible context. Solve that, and everything else starts to fall into place.</p>

2a:T13a2,<p>I have been giving a lot of thought to how we design and structure data architecture to effectively support AI initiatives.</p>
<p>Here is something that hit me: <strong>we have been doing it backwards.</strong></p>
<p>For years, we have built data pipelines first, then tried to make AI understand them. But what if we flipped that? What if we designed our architecture to speak AI’s language from day one?</p>
<p>That’s exactly what an ontology-first approach does.</p>
<p>I have used Palantir Foundry to explore the concept.</p>
<h3>It’s Not Just About Moving Data Anymore</h3>
<p>I used to think data transformation was the endgame. Clean it, move it, store it. Done.</p>
<p><strong>But here is the reality: data without context is just noise.</strong></p>
<p>A table of numbers doesn’t tell you if that “John Smith” is a customer, an employee, or both. It doesn’t know that “Chicago” refers to a location, not just a string.</p>
<p>That’s where ontology changes everything.</p>
<h3>The Three Layers That Actually Matter</h3>
<p>Think of building with ontology like constructing a building.</p>
<h3>🧠 The Semantic Layer — Your Blueprint</h3>
<p>This is where you define what exists in your world. Who are the people? What are the assets? How do things connect?</p>
<p>It’s not technical. It’s human. It answers: “What matters to our business?”</p>
<p><strong>Example:</strong> A “Customer” isn’t just row 47 in table_users. It’s a person with relationships, history, and context.</p>
<h3>🔗 The Kinetic Layer — Your Foundation</h3>
<p>This connects your beautiful blueprint to messy reality.</p>
<p>Your SQL tables, APIs, CSV files — they all map into your ontology here. It’s the bridge between “what we want” and “what we have.”</p>
<p>The magic? Full data lineage. You can trace every insight back to its source.</p>
<h3>⚡ The Dynamic Layer — Your Living System</h3>
<p>This is where it gets interesting.</p>
<p>Business rules. Access controls. Workflows. This layer makes your ontology <em>do</em> things.</p>
<ul>
<li>“Can this person access that case?”</li>
<li>“Should this alert trigger?”</li>
<li>“Who needs to approve this?”</li>
</ul>
<p>It’s not static — it evolves with your business.</p>
<h3>Why This Matters Now</h3>
<p>We are not just building dashboards anymore. We are building systems that need to understand and act.</p>
<p><strong>AI agents need context.</strong> They need to know that deleting a “Customer” record has downstream impacts. That certain data is sensitive. That relationships matter.</p>
<p>An ontology gives AI the worldview it needs to be useful, not just accurate.</p>
<h3>The Mindset Shift</h3>
<p>Stop thinking: <em>“How do we structure data for storage?”</em></p>
<p>Start thinking: <em>“How do we model our world so both humans AND machines understand it?”</em></p>
<p>That’s the future of data engineering.</p>

<p><em>This article was originally published at <a href="https://medium.com/@aradsouza/future-of-data-engineering-why-your-ontology-layer-matters-more-than-ever-187f848d288a">https://medium.com/@aradsouza/future-of-data-engineering-why-your-ontology-layer-matters-more-than-ever-187f848d288a</a></em></p>
2b:T2931,<p>The proliferation of Artificial Intelligence (AI) and Machine Learning (ML) across enterprise environments necessitates a dedicated, rigorous security strategy. MLSecOps (Machine Learning Security Operations) provides the necessary framework to embed security throughout the entire AI lifecycle, ensuring systems are resilient, trustworthy, and compliant.</p>
<p>This article details how MLSecOps aligns with established risk management standards and outlines the practical steps for integrating security, from strategic threat modeling to secure deployment.</p>
<h3>Integrating MLSecOps with the NIST AI Risk Management Framework (AI RMF)</h3>
<p>Effective AI governance is anchored in continuous risk management. The <strong>National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) AI Risk Management Framework (AI RMF)</strong> offers a structured approach that perfectly complements MLSecOps practices. This framework is built upon four core, continuous functions: <strong>Map, Measure, Manage, and Govern</strong>.</p>
<p>NIST provides a companion <strong>AI RMF Playbook</strong> to guide organizations in practical adoption, demonstrating that combining the core functions of the AI RMF with MLSecOps principles is essential for building robust, risk-managed AI capabilities.</p>
<h3>Analyzing AI Attack Vectors and Vulnerabilities</h3>
<p>The introduction of AI and ML creates new attack surface areas that are not adequately addressed by traditional cybersecurity frameworks. Organizations must categorize these threats to deploy appropriate defenses.</p>
<p>NIST classifies attacks on machine learning into two broad categories:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Attacks on Predictive AI (Pred-AI):</strong> Systems that analyze historical data to find patterns and forecast potential outcomes (e.g., fraud detection, stock valuation).</li>
<li><strong>Attacks on Generative AI (Gen-AI):</strong> Systems that ingest data to generate new content (e.g., Large Language Models (LLMs), image generators).</li>
</ol>
<p>A prominent threat to Gen-AI is <strong>Prompt Injection Attacks</strong>, which subvert the model’s guardrails or intended function:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Direct Prompt Injection:</strong> The adversary inputs a prompt designed to bypass system restrictions. The “Grandma Jailbreak” is a classic example, where a request framed as an innocuous story (e.g., a bedtime tale about a secret recipe) tricks the AI into revealing sensitive information it was trained to withhold.</li>
<li><strong>Indirect Prompt Injection:</strong> An attacker provides malicious input to System A, which is then processed by the targeted System B. For instance, malicious, invisible text embedded in a webpage can be parsed by an LLM that is being trained on or interacting with that page, causing the AI to later output the attacker’s instruction as fact.</li>
</ul>
<p>These sophisticated vectors underscore the necessity of integrating security practices directly into the ML lifecycle, moving beyond reactive, perimeter-based security.</p>
<h3>The MLSecOps Approach to Threat Modeling</h3>
<p><strong>Threat modeling</strong> is a crucial, iterative activity for risk management, providing a strong foundation for understanding exposures and their potential organizational impacts. For AI/ML systems, the process must be customized to account for data, model, and lifecycle complexities.</p>
<h3>Customizing the AI/ML Threat Model</h3>
<p>Unlike traditional DevSecOps, where training on live or production data is typically discouraged, it is often a <strong>requirement</strong> for ML. Therefore, customized threat models must shift focus from data inclusion risk to <strong>data provenance</strong> and <strong>integrity</strong>:</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*bmmrzDkPKh0Xp-56sc8_mg.png" alt=""></p>
<p>Creating effective, AI-aware threat models requires incorporating specific threats related to the technology itself. Common categories of concern include: <strong>Technique and Processes, Accessibility, Identifiability and Linkability, Security and Safety, Ethics and Human Rights, and Compliance.</strong></p>
<h3>Strategic Threat Analysis</h3>
<p>While customized models focus on technical components, <strong>strategic threat models</strong> address the business risks that span organizational units, ecosystems, or impact the target operating model.</p>
<p>Strategic MLSecOps threat models define and address business threats influenced by AI/ML deployment, taking into account:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Resource Risks:</strong> Given the novelty of MLSecOps, the inability to <strong>staff and manage</strong> a critical AI/ML launch (a resource-related risk) represents a significant threat to the project’s success.</li>
<li><strong>Legal and Regulatory Risks:</strong> What sectoral regulations apply (e.g., healthcare, finance)?</li>
<li><strong>Business Consequence:</strong> What is the financial and <strong>reputational impact</strong> if the AI fails to complete a core customer transaction?</li>
</ul>
<p>Reviewing both strategic and technical threats ensures that deployed AI/ML solutions are robustly secured and aligned with overarching business objectives.</p>
<h3>Ensuring Adversarial Robustness and Secure Deployment</h3>
<p>Threat modeling provides the inputs for creating test plans and strategies to verify that AI/ML systems are <strong>robust and resilient</strong> against intentional and unintentional failures.</p>
<h3>Adversarial Training and Robustness</h3>
<p>Intentional failures, known as <strong>adversarial attacks</strong>, occur when an attacker exploits a system vulnerability. An <strong>evasion attack</strong>, for example, involves slightly modifying the input of a trained model to cause misclassification (e.g., manipulating a stop sign image to be interpreted as a yield sign).</p>
<p><strong>Adversarial training</strong> is the key defense mechanism. This technique involves emulating real-world malicious activity within a <strong>sandboxed environment</strong> to observe system performance under attack.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Perturbation Techniques:</strong> Trainers introduce controlled <strong>perturbations</strong> (noise) to the training data. For instance, changing a few pixels in an image can shift the classification outcome. By integrating these adversarial samples into the training data, engineers increase the model’s <strong>robustness and accuracy</strong> even when facing malicious inputs.</li>
</ul>
<p>Investing in adversarial training fortifies defenses, ensuring reliable AI/ML performance even under duress.</p>
<h3>Secure Model Deployment and Continuous Monitoring</h3>
<p>Once the model has passed rigorous testing, the final phases of the MLSecOps lifecycle are executed: <strong>Release, Deploy, Operate, and Monitor.</strong></p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Release:</strong> This is the final gate. The MLSecOps team confirms performance, validates compliance, and may apply digital signing. The <strong>ML-BOM (Machine Learning Bill of Materials)</strong> is generated, documenting all components for supply chain visibility.</li>
<li><strong>Deploy:</strong> Models become available in production with security policies enforced. Using <strong>Policy as Code</strong> (e.g., OPA), security policies can automatically manage risks — for instance, removing all instances of a model from production if it is flagged as insecure.</li>
<li><strong>Operate:</strong> Models are protected via controls like <strong>rate limiting</strong> to manage requests and prevent <strong>DDoS</strong> attacks. Access controls and segmentation are reviewed and refined based on operational data.</li>
<li><strong>Monitor:</strong> Models are <strong>dynamic</strong> and can experience <strong>drift or decay</strong> — a gradual loss of accuracy over time. Continuous monitoring detects this degradation, providing valuable insights for <strong>retraining or adjustment</strong>. This monitoring closes the MLSecOps loop, feeding real-world data back into the planning phase to continuously improve the security posture.</li>
</ol>
<p>By strategically applying MLSecOps principles throughout the AI lifecycle, organizations can ensure the creation of secure, compliant, and efficient AI/ML applications, effectively managing the unique risks associated with this transformative technology.</p>
<h3>References and Further Reading</h3>
<p>For those looking to dive deeper into the frameworks and tools discussed, the following resources provide detailed guidance for implementing a robust MLSecOps program:</p>
<p>**NIST AI Risk Management Framework (AI RMF 1.0) —**The core framework detailing the Map, Measure, Manage, and Govern functions for deploying trustworthy and responsible AI systems.</p>
<p><a href="https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf">Download the NIST AI RMF 1.0 (PDF)</a></p>
<p><strong>NIST AI RMF Playbook</strong></p>
<p>Companion resource to the RMF, offering actionable advice and constructive guidance for putting the framework’s concepts into practice.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.nist.gov/itl/ai-risk-management-framework/nist-ai-rmf-playbook">Access the NIST AI RMF Playbook</a></p>
<p><strong>Microsoft: Threat Modeling AI/ML Systems and Dependencies</strong></p>
<p>A comprehensive article detailing how to approach threat modeling specifically for AI and ML components, including data, model, and infrastructure risks.</p>
<p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/security/engineering/threat-modeling-aiml">Read the Microsoft Article</a></p>
<p><strong>OWASP Top 10 for Large Language Model Applications (LLMs)</strong></p>

<p><a href="https://owasp.org/www-project-top-10-for-large-language-model-applications/">View the OWASP LLM Top 10</a></p>
<p><strong>PLOT4ai: Quick Assessment Tool (Online)</strong></p>
<p>An online resource that offers a quick assessment feature for users to conduct threat models focusing on ML-specific lifecycle phases and relevant categories.</p>

<p><em>This article was originally published at <a href="https://medium.com/@aradsouza/applying-mlsecops-to-secure-the-ai-lifecycle-be6e0acf9679">https://medium.com/@aradsouza/applying-mlsecops-to-secure-the-ai-lifecycle-be6e0acf9679</a></em></p>
2c:T17b1,<p>Successfully implementing <strong>MLSecOps</strong> requires infusing security practices directly into the existing MLOps pipeline. This strategy, built on the pillars of people, processes, and technology, ensures that AI systems are secured from conception to deployment and beyond.</p>
<h3>Step-by-Step: Infusing MLSecOps into Existing Processes</h3>
<p>MLSecOps activities must be integrated into every phase of the ML lifecycle (scoping, engineering, model development, testing, and release) to ensure continuous security.</p>
<p>Model signing, in particular, is critical, as it provides verifiable cryptographic signatures to ensure the authenticity and integrity of ML models throughout deployment and distribution.</p>
<h3>Foundations for AI/ML Risk Assessments and Assurance</h3>
<p>The MLSecOps approach establishes the technical activities that implement the principles outlined in the <strong>NIST AI Risk Management Framework (AI RMF)</strong>, organizing AI assurance into four key functions: <strong>Map, Measure, Manage, and Govern</strong>.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*N0HD-C2kEYD8cPoL6j5sOQ.png" alt=""></p>
<p>Crucially, <strong>Governance</strong> is a cross-cutting function. By including <strong>AI and ML assets in the organization’s risk register</strong>, these systems are formally tracked alongside other critical business assets. This enables effective monitoring of their performance, vulnerabilities, and potential business impacts, driving proactive risk mitigation and responsible deployment.</p>
<h3>AI Incident Response Plans</h3>
<p>Effective MLSecOps requires extending traditional incident response plans to address AI-specific risks.</p>
<p>Traditional incident response steps (Preparation, Detection, Containment, Eradication, Recovery, Post-Incident Activity) must be augmented with strategies to handle:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Model Failures</strong></li>
<li><strong>Data Drift</strong> (model decay)</li>
<li><strong>Adversarial Machine Learning Attacks</strong> (e.g., model poisoning, evasion).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Key NIST/MLSecOps Actions for AI Incidents</h3>
<p>The NIST AI RMF emphasizes several proactive measures for incident management, typically covered under the <strong>Manage</strong> and <strong>Govern</strong> functions:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Policy Establishment:</strong> Creating policies for <strong>reporting, documenting, and publicly disclosing</strong> AI incidents.</li>
<li><strong>AI-Specific Guidelines:</strong> Establishing guidelines tailored to the risks and performance characteristics of AI systems.</li>
<li><strong>Information Sharing:</strong> Implementing a mechanism to share information about errors, near misses, and attack patterns with incident databases or similar organizations.</li>
</ol>
<h3>The Criticality of Recovery</h3>
<p>The <strong>Recovery</strong> step is paramount in an ML environment. In the event of a compromised ML model, damage can include data breaches, financial losses, or disruption to business operations. Mitigation involves:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Swift Containment:</strong> Promptly removing affected models from production.</li>
<li><strong>Rollback Procedures:</strong> Initiating a rollback to restore the model to its <strong>uncompromised state</strong>. This requires meticulous planning, <strong>version control mechanisms</strong>, and regular backups of model configurations and datasets.</li>
</ul>
<p>Adapting incident response plans to the full AI lifecycle is imperative for ensuring resilience and security in the face of today’s rapidly evolving AI threat landscape.</p>
<h3>Audit, Inventory, and Supply Chain Integrity</h3>
<p>Whether models are built in-house, purchased, or acquired from open-source libraries, they are critical assets. <strong>AI-aware auditing</strong> and comprehensive inventory management provide the visibility needed to manage risks.</p>
<h3>The Role of the ML-BOM and Auditing</h3>
<p>An audit of AI systems utilizes inventory and supply chain information — including system operation, origin, build process, connections, and impact on decision-making.</p>
<p>The <strong>ML-BOM</strong> is the foundation for auditing, capturing:</p>
<ul>
<li>Training data sources and versions.</li>
<li>Algorithms in use.</li>
<li>Model versioning.</li>
<li>Who trained and validated the model.</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Auditing</strong> is essential because it:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Ensures Accountability and Transparency:</strong> Shines a light on potential <strong>bias, vulnerabilities, and errors</strong>, providing stakeholders assurance that decisions are being made fairly.</li>
<li><strong>Strengthens Risk Management:</strong> Proactively identifies and mitigates risks <em>before</em> they escalate into major issues.</li>
<li><strong>Fosters Trust:</strong> Provides insight into the model’s inner workings, helping to identify the precise reason for a failure.</li>
</ol>
<h3>Regulatory Standards and Actions</h3>
<p>Regulatory bodies worldwide are recognizing the need for formal AI risk assurance. Key standards and legislation driving this push include:</p>
<ul>
<li>The <strong>EU AI Act</strong>.</li>
<li><strong>ISO/IEC 5338</strong> on AI system lifecycle processes.</li>
<li><strong>OWASP’s AI Security and Privacy Guide</strong>.</li>
<li>The <strong>US 2023 Executive Order</strong> on safe, secure, and trustworthy AI.</li>
</ul>
<p>To initiate AI/ML auditing, organizations must <strong>create a comprehensive inventory of the entire ML supply chain</strong>, capturing training methodologies, dependencies, and data sources. Consistent and frequent auditing provides immediate visibility, enabling proactive incident prevention and effective risk management.</p>

2d:T20a5,<h3>Why Security Can’t Be an Afterthought in AI Development</h3>
<p>Artificial intelligence is no longer a futuristic concept — it’s embedded in our daily lives. From voice assistants that understand our commands to automated financial systems managing millions of transactions, AI-powered technologies have become indispensable. But as we increasingly rely on these intelligent systems, a critical question emerges: How do we ensure they’re secure and reliable?</p>
<p>The answer lies in <strong>MLSecOps</strong> — Machine Learning Security Operations — a systematic approach to building security into every stage of the AI lifecycle.</p>
<h3>What Exactly Is MLSecOps?</h3>
<p>Credits to ProtectAI — <a href="https://mlsecops.com/what-is-mlsecops">https://mlsecops.com/what-is-mlsecops</a></p>
<p>Think of MLSecOps as the natural evolution of DevSecOps, specifically tailored for the unique challenges of artificial intelligence and machine learning. While DevSecOps brought security into traditional software development, MLSecOps extends these principles to address the distinctive vulnerabilities that come with AI systems.</p>
<p>At its core, MLSecOps integrates security practices throughout the entire machine learning pipeline — from initial data collection and model development to deployment and continuous monitoring. It’s not just about protecting code; it’s about securing data, models, and the complex processes that make AI work.</p>
<h3>Understanding the Foundation: AI vs. ML</h3>
<p>Before we dive deeper, let’s clarify two terms that are often used interchangeably but aren’t quite synonymous:</p>
<p><strong>Artificial Intelligence (AI)</strong> encompasses the broader concept of machines performing tasks that typically require human intelligence — understanding natural language, recognizing patterns, making decisions, and solving complex problems.</p>
<p><strong>Machine Learning (ML)</strong> is a subset of AI that enables systems to learn and improve from data without being explicitly programmed for every scenario.</p>
<p>Consider a major financial institution using AI for fraud detection. The AI system oversees the entire fraud detection process, but it’s the carefully tuned ML models — trained to analyze transaction data and learn spending patterns — that identify unusual behavior and adapt over time. The ML models are the engine that powers the AI’s intelligence.</p>
<h3>Why MLSecOps Matters Now More Than Ever</h3>
<p>The consequences of unsecured AI systems aren’t theoretical — they’re potentially catastrophic:</p>
<p><strong>In Transportation:</strong> Imagine hackers manipulating an autonomous vehicle’s decision-making process. The safety implications are immediate and severe.</p>
<p><strong>In Healthcare:</strong> An AI diagnostic system compromised by malicious actors could lead to incorrect medical treatments, putting lives at risk.</p>
<p><strong>In Finance:</strong> A corrupted AI trading algorithm could trigger market manipulation, regulatory violations, and massive financial losses.</p>
<p>These aren’t scare tactics — they’re realistic scenarios that organizations must prepare for. AI and ML systems face threats at multiple levels, from supply chain vulnerabilities to sophisticated adversarial attacks designed to manipulate model outcomes.</p>
<h3>The Evolution: From DevOps to MLSecOps</h3>
<p>The journey to MLSecOps follows a clear evolutionary path:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>DevOps</strong> automated software development and deployment</li>
<li><strong>DevSecOps</strong> introduced security early in the development process — the “Shift Left” principle</li>
<li><strong>MLOps</strong> adapted DevOps practices for machine learning’s unique challenges</li>
<li><strong>MLSecOps</strong> brings security into MLOps, addressing AI-specific threats</li>
</ol>
<p>What makes ML different from traditional software? Unlike conventional applications that primarily deal with code, ML systems involve complex data handling, continuous model training, and dynamic learning processes. These introduce unique vulnerabilities that standard DevSecOps practices don’t fully address.</p>
<h3>The Five Pillars of MLSecOps Assurance</h3>
<p>Effective MLSecOps is built on five critical categories of assurance:</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*W5uWhpwX8g3Vi_V6tmySBg.png" alt=""></p>
<h3>1. Supply Chain Vulnerability</h3>
<p>AI supply chains are complex ecosystems. A vulnerability anywhere in the chain — from data sources to pre-trained models downloaded from repositories — can be exploited to compromise entire systems.</p>
<h3>2. Model Provenance</h3>
<p>With 80% of ML and analytics artifacts being open source, tracking a model’s complete history — its development, modifications, and deployment — is essential for identifying potential security issues.</p>
<h3>3. Governance, Risk, and Compliance (GRC)</h3>
<p>Strong governance ensures responsible ML use through regular audits assessing fairness, transparency, and accountability. The Machine Learning Bill of Materials (ML-BOM) provides a comprehensive inventory of all components used in model development, helping identify vulnerabilities.</p>
<h3>4. Trusted AI</h3>
<p>AI systems must be fair, unbiased, and explainable. We need AI to make equitable decisions regardless of race, gender, age, or other characteristics, without perpetuating existing biases. Transparency and explainability are key to achieving trust.</p>
<h3>5. Adversarial Machine Learning</h3>
<p>This field focuses on understanding and defending against attacks on ML systems — from manipulating input data to cause incorrect predictions to altering models themselves to reduce accuracy or cause unexpected behaviors.</p>
<h3>Building a Successful MLSecOps Program</h3>
<p>Implementing MLSecOps isn’t just about technology — it’s about people, processes, and tools working together. Here are the essential strategies:</p>
<p><strong>Assemble a Cross-Functional Team:</strong> Break down silos between data scientists, security professionals, and operations teams. MLSecOps requires collaboration across disciplines.</p>
<p><strong>Invest in Education:</strong> Provide hands-on training to help team members understand ML-specific security risks and mitigation strategies.</p>
<p><strong>Deploy ML-Aware Tools:</strong> Traditional security tools weren’t designed for ML contexts. Invest in or develop tools that understand the unique risks of machine learning systems.</p>
<p><strong>Implement Policy as Code:</strong> Automate security policies specifically for ML models to ensure consistent enforcement across all deployments.</p>
<p><strong>Prioritize Privacy and Data Security:</strong> Given ML’s dependence on data, protecting sensitive information throughout the pipeline is paramount.</p>
<p><strong>Monitor Continuously:</strong> ML models in production need ongoing surveillance. Continuous monitoring provides feedback that improves security across the entire MLSecOps loop.</p>
<h3>The Path Forward</h3>
<p>As AI and ML continue to transform industries, the risks associated with these technologies will only grow more complex. Organizations can no longer treat security as an afterthought or bolt-on feature. MLSecOps represents a fundamental shift in how we approach AI development — embedding security from the very beginning.</p>
<p>The question isn’t whether your organization should adopt MLSecOps practices, but how quickly you can implement them. In a world where AI systems make critical decisions affecting safety, health, and financial security, building inherently secure AI isn’t just a best practice — it’s an imperative.</p>
<p>By embracing MLSecOps, organizations can harness the transformative potential of AI while ensuring these systems remain secure, reliable, and trustworthy. The future of AI is bright, but only if we build it on a foundation of security.</p>

<p><em>This article was originally published at <a href="https://medium.com/@aradsouza/mlsecops-securing-the-future-of-ai-and-machine-learning-61e9004e1a10">https://medium.com/@aradsouza/mlsecops-securing-the-future-of-ai-and-machine-learning-61e9004e1a10</a></em></p>
2e:T2055,<p>In the age of AI, software security isn’t just about code — it’s about algorithms, data pipelines, and complex models. This shift demands a radical evolution from traditional DevSecOps to <strong>MLSecOps</strong>, which requires a uniquely skilled, cross-functional team. Building security into your AI ecosystem isn’t a job for one department; it’s a team sport.</p>
<p>Here’s how to build your MLSecOps Dream Team and foster the cross-collaboration necessary for success.</p>
<h3>Building the Team: Ownership and Key Roles</h3>
<p>MLSecOps, like any robust security strategy, relies on the pillars of <strong>people, process, and technology</strong>. The “people” component requires a roster specifically tailored to the nuances of Artificial Intelligence.</p>
<p>We can divide the key stakeholders into four essential groups:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>ML Practitioners:</strong> This group — including <strong>ML Engineers, Data Scientists, and ML Architects</strong> — owns the integrity and function of the AI assets. They are responsible for the design, development, maintenance, and reliability of the models and data pipelines.</li>
<li><strong>Security Experts:</strong> These team members (e.g., security architects, threat analysts) help the larger team understand threats specific to AI/ML, the associated risks, and recommended mitigations. They translate traditional cybersecurity principles into the ML context.</li>
<li><strong>Subject Matter Experts (SMEs):</strong> This group understands the business or industry context. Roles like <strong>UX Designers</strong> ensure that security measures don’t compromise the user experience, while other SMEs align security with overall organizational goals.</li>
<li><strong>Regulatory Experts:</strong> Team members from <strong>Legal, Compliance, and HR</strong> are crucial for navigating the complex landscape of regulations, compliance mandates, and ethical guidelines, safeguarding AI initiatives from legal or social pitfalls.</li>
</ol>
<p>This diversity is vital because AI security requires bridging the knowledge gap between technical security and machine learning science. ML practitioners understand model intricacies but may miss traditional threat vectors, while security experts know vulnerabilities but might not grasp how a model’s core components are susceptible to attack. The Dream Team unites these essential, yet often separate, objectives.</p>
<h3>The Violet Teaming Integrative Framework</h3>
<p>If you’re familiar with the cyber security concept of <strong>Purple Teaming</strong> (combining offensive Red Team strategies with defensive Blue Team strategies), <strong>Violet Teaming</strong> is the evolution tailored for AI.</p>
<p>Introduced by Dr. Alexander Titus, <strong>Violet Teaming</strong> is an integrative framework that expands the collaboration beyond red and blue. It brings in viewpoints from a wider array of stakeholders to address risks related to <strong>impact, privacy, usability, and ethics</strong>.</p>
<p>Violet Teaming isn’t just about finding technical vulnerabilities; it’s a holistic strategy that focuses on <strong>“building security in”</strong> at every stage.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Expanded Viewpoint:</strong> By converging expertise from ML engineers, security experts, privacy lawyers, and ethics experts, the team gains a complete perspective.</li>
<li><strong>Novel Risk Identification:</strong> A privacy lawyer, for example, might ask about the potential for <strong>de-anonymization</strong> or how a data lakehouse could reconstitute sensitive data, concerns that might not be top-of-mind for a security engineer focused on network intrusion.</li>
<li><strong>Ethical and Social Guardrails:</strong> This approach ensures the team considers non-technical risks like the social and ethical impacts of algorithmic bias, securing the system not just against hackers, but against unintended societal harm.</li>
</ul>
<p>Adopting the violet teaming concept is a powerful tool for defining your organization’s needs and structuring your MLSecOps Dream Team.</p>
<h3>Facilitating Cross-Collaboration for Implementation</h3>
<p>The success of MLSecOps hinges on dissolving organizational silos. The foundational partnership is between <strong>Security</strong> and <strong>ML practitioners</strong>.</p>
<h3>Collaboration in Practice</h3>
<p>To operationalize this partnership, organizations need a culture of knowledge sharing:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Joint Inventory and Scans:</strong> Security and ML teams must collaborate to create a full <strong>Machine Learning Bill of Materials (ML-BOM)</strong> and jointly conduct model security scans.</li>
<li><strong>Threat Modeling:</strong> Security professionals should integrate ML expertise into their threat modeling sessions to foresee attacks like model poisoning or evasion.</li>
<li><strong>Incident Response:</strong> ML practitioners benefit from a security team’s insights into data privacy, incident response protocols, and regulatory compliance.</li>
</ul>
<h3>Beyond Security and ML</h3>
<p>Cross-collaboration must extend to other key stakeholders:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Legal:</strong> Provides crucial insights into <strong>regulatory compliance</strong> (e.g., geographic data laws, consequences of algorithmic bias). They ensure all AI deployments mitigate legal risks.</li>
<li><strong>Human Resources (HR):</strong> Ensures personnel receive the necessary training to use AI/ML responsibly, helping employees understand <strong>ethical implications</strong> and data privacy best practices. This awareness reduces unintentional misuse and fosters a culture of responsible AI.</li>
<li><strong>Procurement:</strong> Works closely with the security team to ensure that the selection and integration of <strong>third-party AI solutions</strong> (e.g., purchasing a commercial LLM) align with the organization’s risk tolerance and security standards.</li>
</ul>
<p>The effectiveness of MLSecOps is a direct reflection of this organizational unity. By championing cross-collaboration, you ensure your MLSecOps Dream Team operates with clarity, agility, and unity.</p>
<h3>Empowering Stakeholders with Team Training</h3>
<p>Training is the engine that drives this cross-collaboration. Investing in targeted, interdisciplinary training sessions ensures all stakeholders are equipped for the MLSecOps journey.</p>
<p>Here are suggested training topics:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>AI/ML Threat Landscape Awareness:</strong> Led by internal or external experts, this training covers emerging threats, supply chain vulnerabilities, and AI-specific attack vectors (e.g., data poisoning). The goal is for every team member to be able to <strong>see the threats, know the risks, and plan for management.</strong></li>
<li><strong>Interdisciplinary Workshops:</strong> Host joint sessions that bring together security, ML, legal, and HR teams to discuss shared AI security challenges and foster a deeper understanding of each other’s roles.</li>
<li><strong>Regulatory Compliance Workshops:</strong> Led by the legal team, these sessions educate the MLSecOps team on specific regulatory requirements and new laws related to AI in your operational jurisdiction.</li>
<li><strong>Ethical AI Training:</strong> This pivotal component covers AI ethics principles, responsible usage, and the impact of algorithmic decisions on individuals and society (e.g., credit worthiness, hiring decisions).</li>
<li><strong>Incident Response Drills:</strong> Conducting joint simulations of real-world AI incidents (e.g., a data leakage event triggered by a prompt injection) helps identify gaps in inter-departmental response procedures.</li>
</ul>
<p>Empowering your stakeholders through continuous training is the final step in solidifying your MLSecOps Dream Team, ensuring your organization is proactively secured against the evolving risks of artificial intelligence.</p>

<h3><strong>High Level Architecture</strong></h3>
<p>This below diagram provides comprehensive overview of the dbt CI/CD system architecture, detailing how automated pipelines, code quality systems, and infrastructure components work together to enable reliable data transformation development and deployment. The architecture implements enterprise-grade practices including isolated testing environments, state-aware deployments, and multi-layered quality assurance.</p>
<h3>Pipeline Architecture Overview</h3>
<p>The CI/CD system implements a two-stage pipeline architecture with clear separation between validation and deployment concerns.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*M30rTSggIXh7FyHft3s4mg.png" alt=""></p>
<h3><strong>CI Pipeline</strong></h3>
<p>The CI pipeline serves as the primary quality gate for all code changes, implementing a multi-stage validation process that prevents broken code from reaching production. The pipeline runs automatically on pushes to development branches and pull requests, providing immediate feedback to developers.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*jPwPfnHR8hZ13jCi6DBm9g.png" alt=""></p>
<h4>Trigger Conditions and Concurrency Management</h4>
<p>The pipeline uses sophisticated trigger conditions and concurrency controls to optimize resource usage and provide fast feedback.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*kRvbpPP8P4sT0ZxJpfh1yA.png" alt=""><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*lz9GfyJDMCvBYtlB1y_eGg.png" alt=""></p>
<h4>Dynamic Schema Management</h4>
<p>One of the most sophisticated features of the CI pipeline is its dynamic schema creation for test isolation.</p>
<p>Schema Generation Logic The dynamic schema naming is implemented.</p>
<ul>
<li>Pull Requests: <code>pr_{PR_NUMBER}_{COMMIT_SHA}</code></li>
<li>Branch Pushes: <code>{BRANCH_NAME}_{COMMIT_SHA}</code> (with special character sanitization)</li>
</ul>
<p>Cleanup Process The pipeline ensures proper resource cleanup by always running the schema cleanup step even if the build fails:</p>
<pre><code>dbt run-operation drop_schema --args &#x27;{ &quot;schema_name&quot;: &quot;${{ env.DBT_SCHEMA }}&quot; }&#x27;
</code></pre><p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*1XstYNqTbjsqefNphWSV0w.png" alt=""></p>
<h4>dbt Build and Test Execution</h4>
<p>The core dbt validation job runs the complete build process in an isolated environment.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*ygOihQus9G8Szohut6eHsA.png" alt=""></p>
<h3><strong>CD Pipeline</strong></h3>
<p>The Continuous Deployment Pipeline is responsible for deploying validated dbt changes to the production environment. This system automatically triggers when code is merged to the main branch, performing state-aware deployments that only build modified models and their downstream dependencies.</p>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*ioTEx_6t90FscvTMcRK_iw.png" alt=""></p>
<h4><strong>State-Aware Deployment Strategy</strong></h4>
<p>The pipeline implements intelligent deployment logic that minimizes execution time by only building modified models.</p>
<h4>Manifest Download and State Detection</h4>
<p>The deployment strategy uses the <code>Legit-Labs/action-download-artifact@v2</code> action to retrieve the previous production manifest:</p>
<ul>
<li>Artifact Name: <code>dbt-manifest-prod</code></li>
<li>Path: <code>prod-artifacts</code> directory</li>
<li>Workflow Source: <code>dbt-cd-pipeline.yml</code></li>
<li>Continue on Error: <code>true</code> (gracefully handles first deployment)</li>
</ul>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*8nVwmhkJ1vmxSxy_6r0Uxw.png" alt=""></p>
<h4>Build Execution Logic</h4>
<p>The build step implements conditional logic for optimal deployment:</p>
<pre><code># State-based build (when previous manifest exists)
</code></pre><p>This approach:</p>
<ul>
<li>Reduces deployment time by only building changed models and their dependencies</li>
<li>Maintains data consistency through the <code>state:modified+</code> selector</li>
<li>Enables safe rollback by deferring to previous state when possible</li>
<li>Handles first deployment gracefully with full build fallback</li>
</ul>
<h3>Artifact Management System</h3>
<p>The pipeline maintains deployment state through comprehensive artifact management.</p>
<h4><strong>Artifact Upload Strategy</strong></h4>
<p><img src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/800/1*78x3rmKcuBmxxBHMbkvMLg.png" alt=""></p>
<h4>Manifest Artifact</h4>
<ul>
<li>Name: <code>dbt-manifest-prod</code></li>
<li>Path: <code>target/manifest.json</code></li>
<li>Condition: Only on successful builds</li>
<li>Retention: 7 days</li>
<li>Purpose: Enables state-based deployments for subsequent runs</li>
</ul>
<h4>Results Artifact</h4>
<ul>
<li>Name: <code>dbt-results</code></li>
<li>Path: <code>dbt_results.md</code></li>
<li>Condition: Always uploaded (success or failure)</li>
<li>Retention: 7 days</li>
<li>Purpose: Provides deployment logs and debugging information</li>
</ul>

30:T1637,<blockquote>
<p>Choosing Your Historical Data Strategy: Comparing dbt Snapshots and CDC-Driven Custom SCD2</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Slowly Changing Dimensions (SCD Type 2) are foundational for tracking historical data in analytics and compliance. In modern data stacks, two dominant approaches emerge: dbt Snapshots and Custom SCD2 logic (especially enhanced with Change Data Capture, or CDC). Each serves different needs, and understanding their differences is critical for making the right choice in data engineering.</p>
<h3>What Are dbt Snapshots?</h3>
<p>dbt Snapshots are a declarative configuration within dbt that automatically version row-level changes in a source table over time. When a change is detected during each dbt run, Snapshots create new entries, managing validity intervals (via <code>dbt_valid_from</code> and <code>dbt_valid_to</code>) and maintaining a “history” without custom CDC logic or extensive SQL development.</p>
<ul>
<li>Architecture: Select query + YAML snapshot configuration.</li>
<li>Automation: dbt manages inserts/updates, tracks changes, and versioning in snapshot tables.</li>
<li>Efficiency: Best suited for analytical, batch workloads where near real-time tracking isn’t required.</li>
</ul>
<h3>What Is Custom SCD2 Logic (with CDC)?</h3>
<p>Custom SCD2 Logic involves manually written SQL models that implement Type 2 behavior — new rows for each change, explicit “valid from/to” columns, versioning logic, and, often, CDC integrations for higher granularity and timeliness.</p>
<ul>
<li>CDC Integration: CDC tracks each source change in real time (or near-real time), often parsing database logs, triggers, or streams to collect every data change and send those as events to downstream systems.</li>
<li>Flexibility: Engineers can design bespoke change detection, apply custom business rules, and extend beyond Type 2 for advanced audit/compliance requirements.</li>
<li>Complexity and Cost: Requires orchestration, monitoring, development, and additional infrastructure.</li>
</ul>
<h3>Technical and Functional Comparison</h3>
<h3>When Best to Use Snapshot and Custom SCD Type 2 Logic</h3>
<h4>dbt Snapshot Works Best When:</h4>
<ul>
<li>Historical analysis is performed in scheduled batches</li>
<li>Data changes infrequently</li>
<li>Simplicity, speed to implementation, and low operational overhead are prioritized</li>
<li>Example: Monthly reporting on customer tier, annual compliance audits</li>
</ul>
<h4>Custom SCD2 with CDC Is Better When:</h4>
<ul>
<li>High change velocity, frequent data mutability</li>
<li>Regulatory environments requiring full transactional history and precise audit trails</li>
<li>Operational dashboards and real-time systems powering product features</li>
<li>Schema must be strictly validated at every layer</li>
<li>Example: Financial transactions, healthcare records, fraud detection analytics</li>
</ul>
<h3>Final Thoughts</h3>
<p>dbt Snapshots and Custom SCD2 with CDC both enable SCD2, but their design philosophies differ:</p>
<ul>
<li>dbt Snapshots are ideal for analytical, batch-driven, lower-complexity environments with moderate history needs.</li>
<li>Custom SCD2 with CDC deliver best-in-class historical fidelity, real-time data, and strict contract enforcement for high-compliance and operational workloads.</li>
</ul>
<p>Choosing between them depends on the organization’s requirements for change granularity, cost, compliance, and engineering investment. Carefully consider your business needs — and the full data contract lifecycle — before architecting your solution.</p>

<p><em>This article was originally published at <a href="https://medium.com/@aradsouza/dbt-snapshot-vs-custom-scd2-with-cdc-comparative-analysis-e34e48168973">https://medium.com/@aradsouza/dbt-snapshot-vs-custom-scd2-with-cdc-comparative-analysis-e34e48168973</a></em></p>
31:T329d,<p><em>Building robust data warehouses that preserve historical context</em></p>
<p>Building a custom SCD Type 2 model in dbt is a powerful way to track historical changes without relying solely on the built-in <code>snapshot</code> functionality, giving you more control over the logic. While dbt snapshots are a great, simple solution for many use cases, a custom approach is often necessary when handling more complex scenarios, such as specific merge strategies, backfilling historical data, or dealing with source systems that lack a reliable <code>updated_at</code> column.</p>
<p>The core of the custom logic involves a few key steps within a dbt model materialized as an <code>incremental</code> table.</p>
<h3>Step-by-Step Logic for Custom SCD Type 2</h3>
<p>Your dbt model will use the <code>incremental</code> materialization, which allows dbt to smartly update and insert records based on your logic, rather than rebuilding the entire table every time. Here&#39;s a breakdown of the SQL logic you&#39;d write:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Identify New and Changed Records:</strong> You’ll start by selecting all records from your source table (e.g., <code>stg_customers</code>). A common and robust way to detect changes is to create a hash of the columns you want to track. The <code>dbt-utils</code> package has a handy <code>surrogate_key</code> macro for this. If the hash of a new record is different from the hash of the latest version of that record in your dimension table, you know a change has occurred.</li>
<li><strong>Expire Old Records:</strong> This is the most critical part of the SCD Type 2 logic. For every record in your source data that has a new, different version (detected in the previous step), you need to “close out” the old, currently active record in your dimension table. You do this by setting its <code>end_date</code> to the timestamp of the new record&#39;s arrival and changing its <code>active_flag</code> from <code>Y</code> to <code>N</code>.</li>
<li><strong>Insert New Records:</strong> For every new or changed record from your source data, you’ll insert a new row into your dimension table. This new row will have the updated information, a <code>start_date</code> set to the current timestamp, and the <code>end_date</code> set to a future value (like &#39;9999-12-31&#39;) to indicate it&#39;s the current, active version. Its <code>active_flag</code> will be <code>Y</code>.</li>
</ol>
<p>This process ensures that your dimension table is always up-to-date with the latest information, while also preserving a full, historical timeline of every change. The <code>dbt-utils</code> package and <code>incremental</code> materialization make writing this custom logic much simpler than writing raw SQL.</p>
<p>Let’s illustrate this with a simple example:</p>
<h3>Before Change:</h3>
<pre><code>customer_id | name | city | active_flag | start_date | end_date
1001 | John Smith | New York | Y | 2023-01-01 | 9999-12-31
</code></pre><h3>After John moves to Boston:</h3>
<pre><code>customer_id | name | city | active_flag | start_date | end_date
1001 | John Smith | New York | N | 2023-01-01 | 2023-06-15
1001 | John Smith | Boston | Y | 2023-06-15 | 9999-12-31
</code></pre><h3>dbt Implementation Architecture</h3>
<p>Our SCD Type 2 implementation follows a three-layer architecture:</p>
<pre><code>Source System (CRM) → Staging Layer → Intermediate Layer → Mart Layer
</code></pre><h3>1. The Staging Layer</h3>
<p>First, we extract raw customer data from TechMart’s CRM system:</p>
<pre><code>-- models/staging/stg_crm__customers.sql
select
 customer_id,
 row_id,
 customer_name,
 first_name,
 last_name,
 email_address,
 phone_number,
 annual_income,
 city,
 marketing_consent_email,
 marketing_consent_sms,
 contact_preference,
 last_modified_date,
 operation_type -- INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE
from {{ source(&#x27;crm&#x27;, &#x27;customers&#x27;) }}
</code></pre><h3>2. The Intermediate Layer</h3>
<p>Here we add SCD-specific logic and generate unique identifiers:</p>
<pre><code>-- models/intermediate/int_crm__customers_scd.sql
select
 *,
 {{ dbt.generate_surrogate_key([
 &#x27;customer_id&#x27;, 
 &#x27;customer_name&#x27;, 
 &#x27;email_address&#x27;,
 &#x27;phone_number&#x27;,
 &#x27;city&#x27;,
 &#x27;marketing_consent_email&#x27;
 ]) }} as scd_id,
 coalesce(last_modified_date, current_timestamp) as effective_start_date
from {{ ref(&#x27;stg_crm__customers&#x27;) }}
</code></pre><h3>3. The Mart Layer — The SCD Type 2 Magic</h3>
<p>Now comes the complex part — the actual SCD Type 2 implementation:</p>
<pre><code>-- models/marts/dim_customers.sql
{{
 config(
 materialized=&#x27;incremental&#x27;,
 unique_key=&#x27;scd_id&#x27;,
 incremental_strategy=&#x27;delete+insert&#x27;
 )
}}

with source_rows as (
 select
 customer_id,
 customer_name,
 first_name,
 last_name,
 email_address,
 phone_number,
 annual_income,
 city,
 marketing_consent_email,
 marketing_consent_sms,
 contact_preference,
 effective_start_date,
 null as effective_end_date,
 scd_id
 from {{ ref(&#x27;int_crm__customers_scd&#x27;) }}
)

{% if is_incremental() %}
 , destination_rows as (
 select
 customer_id,
 customer_name,
 first_name,
 last_name,
 email_address,
 phone_number,
 annual_income,
 city,
 marketing_consent_email,
 marketing_consent_sms,
 contact_preference,
 effective_start_date,
 effective_end_date,
 scd_id
 from {{ this }} 
 where active_flag = &#x27;Y&#x27;
 )

 , new_valid_to as (
 select
 d.customer_id,
 s.effective_start_date as effective_end_date
 from source_rows s
 join destination_rows d
 on s.customer_id = d.customer_id
 and s.scd_id != d.scd_id

 )

 , add_new_valid_to as (
 select
 d.customer_id,
 d.customer_name,
 d.first_name,
 d.last_name,
 d.email_address,
 d.phone_number,
 d.annual_income,
 d.city,
 d.marketing_consent_email,
 d.marketing_consent_sms,
 d.contact_preference,
 d.effective_start_date,
 n.effective_end_date,
 d.scd_id,
 case when n.effective_end_date is null then &#x27;Y&#x27; else &#x27;N&#x27; end as active_flag
 from destination_rows d
 left join new_valid_to n
 on d.customer_id = n.customer_id

 )

 select
 customer_id,
 customer_name,
 first_name,
 last_name,
 email_address,
 phone_number,
 annual_income,
 city,
 marketing_consent_email,
 marketing_consent_sms,
 contact_preference,
 effective_start_date,
 effective_end_date,
 active_flag,
 scd_id
 from add_new_valid_to n
 union
{% endif %}

select
 s.customer_id,
 s.customer_name,
 s.first_name,
 s.last_name,
 s.email_address,
 s.phone_number,
 s.annual_income,
 s.city,
 s.marketing_consent_email,
 s.marketing_consent_sms,
 s.contact_preference,
 s.effective_start_date,
 coalesce(
 lead(s.effective_start_date, 1) over (
 partition by s.customer_id
 order by s.effective_start_date
 ),
 &#x27;9999-12-31&#x27;::timestamp
 ) as effective_end_date,
 case
 when lead(s.effective_start_date, 1) over (
 partition by s.customer_id
 order by s.effective_start_date
 ) is null then &#x27;Y&#x27;
 else &#x27;N&#x27;
 end as active_flag,
 s.scd_id
from source_rows s
</code></pre><h3>Breaking Down the Complex Logic</h3>
<p>Let’s examine the key components:</p>
<h3>1. Window Functions for Temporal Logic</h3>
<p>The heart of SCD Type 2 lies in understanding temporal relationships:</p>
<pre><code>lead(effective_start_date) over (
 partition by customer_id 
 order by effective_start_date
)
</code></pre><p>This window function looks at the <strong>next</strong> record for the same customer. If there’s no next record, this is the current active version.</p>
<h3>2. Active Flag Determination</h3>
<pre><code>case
 when lead(effective_start_date) over (...) is null then &#x27;Y&#x27;
 else &#x27;N&#x27;
end as active_flag
</code></pre><p>Only the latest record for each customer gets an active flag of ‘Y’.</p>
<h3>3. Effective End Date Calculation</h3>
<pre><code>coalesce(
 lead(effective_start_date) over (...), 
 &#x27;9999-12-31&#x27;
) as effective_end_date
</code></pre><p>The end date is either the start date of the next record, or our “forever” date (9999–12–31).</p>
<h3>Incremental Processing</h3>
<p>The real complexity emerges in incremental runs. We need to:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Identify</strong> existing active records that need to be closed</li>
<li><strong>Update</strong> their end dates and active flags</li>
<li><strong>Insert</strong> new records from the source</li>
</ol>
<pre><code>{% if is_incremental() %}
 , destination_rows as (
 select
 customer_id,
 customer_name,
 first_name,
 last_name,
 email_address,
 phone_number,
 annual_income,
 city,
 marketing_consent_email,
 marketing_consent_sms,
 contact_preference,
 effective_start_date,
 effective_end_date,
 scd_id
 from {{ this }} 
 where active_flag = &#x27;Y&#x27;
 )

 , new_valid_to as (
 select
 d.customer_id,
 s.effective_start_date as effective_end_date
 from source_rows s
 join destination_rows d
 on s.customer_id = d.customer_id
 and s.scd_id != d.scd_id
 )

 , add_new_valid_to as (
 select
 d.customer_id,
 d.customer_name,
 d.first_name,
 d.last_name,
 d.email_address,
 d.phone_number,
 d.annual_income,
 d.city,
 d.marketing_consent_email,
 d.marketing_consent_sms,
 d.contact_preference,
 d.effective_start_date,
 n.effective_end_date,
 d.scd_id,
 case when n.effective_end_date is null then &#x27;Y&#x27; else &#x27;N&#x27; end as active_flag
 from destination_rows d
 left join new_valid_to n
 on d.customer_id = n.customer_id
 )

 select
 customer_id,
 customer_name,
 first_name,
 last_name,
 email_address,
 phone_number,
 annual_income,
 city,
 marketing_consent_email,
 marketing_consent_sms,
 contact_preference,
 effective_start_date,
 effective_end_date,
 active_flag,
 scd_id
 from add_new_valid_to n
 union
{% endif %}
</code></pre><p>Here’s a brief breakdown of each part:</p>
<ul>
<li><code>destination_rows</code>: This section queries the existing dimension table (<code>{{ this }}</code>) to find all currently <strong>active records</strong> (<code>where active_flag = &#39;Y&#39;</code>). It&#39;s the &quot;before&quot; snapshot of the most recent data.</li>
<li><code>new_valid_to</code>: This is the core logic for expiring old records. It performs a <code>JOIN</code> between the new incoming data (<code>source_rows</code>) and the current active records (<code>destination_rows</code>). It identifies where a <code>customer_id</code> matches but the <code>scd_id</code> (the hash of the record) is different. This tells the model, &quot;A new version of this customer record exists, so the old one needs to be ended.&quot; It then sets the <code>effective_end_date</code> for the old record to be the <code>effective_start_date</code> of the new one.</li>
<li><code>add_new_valid_to</code>: This part takes all of the currently active records (<code>destination_rows</code>) and &quot;adds&quot; the new <code>effective_end_date</code> to them, based on the results from <code>new_valid_to</code>. It also updates the <code>active_flag</code> to <code>&#39;N&#39;</code> for any records that are about to be expired.</li>
<li><code>union</code>: The final <code>select</code> statement combines two datasets: the records from <code>add_new_valid_to</code> (which now includes both expired and still-active records) and the brand new records from <code>source_rows</code>. This union creates the complete result set that dbt will use to <code>delete+insert</code> and replace the data in the final table. This ensures the historical integrity is maintained by correctly closing out old records and adding new ones.</li>
</ul>

1001 | John Smith | j.smith@new.com | New York | N | 2023-01-30 | 2023-03-01

32:T1b26,<p>Ever feel like your AI projects are hitting a wall, even when the tech seems fine? Often, it’s not the AI itself, but the hidden “<strong>trust erosion</strong>” in your data that’s quietly derailing everything.</p>
<p>It’s about the vital data foundation beneath your shiny AI. The companies winning with AI aren’t the ones with perfect data. They’re the ones with resilient systems that catch imperfections early and respond by design.</p>
<p>Here are four signals your data trust is crumbling, and how to shore it up with practical steps and tools.</p>
<h3>1. Data Discovery Breakdown 🕵️‍♀️</h3>
<p>Your team spends more time playing data detective than actually using data. Finding the right, trustworthy dataset feels like a treasure hunt without a map. This inefficiency kills trust and innovation.</p>
<p><strong>The Fix:</strong> Invest in <strong>metadata management</strong> and <strong>data catalogs</strong>. These tools create a searchable inventory of all your data assets, including information about what the data is, where it comes from, and who owns it. This empowers your teams to quickly find and understand the data they need, replacing guesswork with confidence.</p>
<p><strong>Tools to Explore:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Collibra:</strong> A comprehensive data governance platform that helps you manage, govern, and trust your data by providing features for data classification, privacy, quality, and lineage.</li>
<li><strong>Alation:</strong> A data intelligence platform that combines data cataloging, data governance, and analytics enablement to help teams find, understand, and use data effectively.</li>
<li><strong>Atlan:</strong> A data democratization company that helps teams collaborate on data projects by creating a home for data teams to democratize both internal and external data while automating repetitive tasks.</li>
</ul>
<h3>2. Reactive Data Quality 🚨</h3>
<p>You’re constantly putting out “data quality fires” after issues hit your customers or reports. A marketing campaign goes wrong, a dashboard breaks… the damage is done and trust is shaken. This reactive approach is inefficient and costly.</p>
<p><strong>The Fix:</strong> Deploy <strong>automated data observability frameworks</strong>. These frameworks act like a health monitor for your data, continuously checking its freshness, volume, distribution, and consistency. By catching anomalies and other issues as they happen, you can be proactive, not just reactive, and fix problems before they impact your business.</p>
<p><strong>Tools to Explore:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Monte Carlo:</strong> A data observability platform that helps you prevent bad data from reaching customers by monitoring and alerting on data issues in real time.</li>
<li><strong>Soda:</strong> A data reliability and observability platform that helps data teams discover, prioritize, and resolve data quality issues.</li>
<li><strong>Acceldata:</strong> An enterprise data observability cloud that helps you monitor and manage the reliability, quality, and performance of data systems.</li>
</ul>
<h3>3. Invisible Data Contracts 🤝</h3>
<p>Your data dependencies rely on unwritten rules and tribal knowledge between teams. When a new system rolls out or someone leaves, everything breaks because those “understood” agreements vanish. This lack of clear, explicit agreements leads to brittle data pipelines and frequent failures.</p>
<p><strong>The Fix:</strong> Establish explicit, versioned <strong>data contracts</strong>. A data contract is a formal agreement between data producers and consumers that defines the schema, quality rules, and ownership of a dataset. By formalizing these agreements and embedding them directly into your data pipelines, you make dependencies visible and predictable.</p>
<p><strong>Tools to Explore:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Datafold:</strong> A data diff platform that helps teams prevent data quality issues by automatically comparing datasets and ensuring data contracts are being met.</li>
<li><strong>Pact:</strong> While Pact is more commonly known for consumer-driven contract testing in microservices, its principles can be applied to data contracts to ensure that data producers and consumers have a shared understanding of data structures.</li>
<li><strong>Great Expectations</strong> and <strong>dbt</strong>: These open-source tools can be paired with schema registries to formalize data definitions and quality rules, embedding them directly into your data pipelines for automated validation.</li>
</ul>
<h3>4. Upstream Model Drifts 🕰️</h3>
<p>Your once-brilliant AI model that predicted churn perfectly suddenly starts making mistakes. Subtle, unmonitored changes in the input data have silently eroded its accuracy, wasting your AI investment. This phenomenon, known as <strong>data drift</strong>, is a silent killer of model performance.</p>
<p><strong>The Fix:</strong> Give your AI a regular health check-up! Integrate <strong>model monitoring</strong> directly with your <strong>data observability</strong>. This allows you to track not only the performance of your AI models but also the health of the data they are trained on and using for predictions. By monitoring for data drift and other changes, you can retrain or update your models before their performance degrades.</p>
<p><strong>Tools to Explore:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Datadog</strong> and <strong>Grafana:</strong> These monitoring tools can be configured to track model performance metrics and alerts.</li>
<li><strong>MLflow:</strong> An open-source platform for managing the end-to-end machine learning lifecycle, including experiment tracking and model management.</li>
<li><strong>Arize AI</strong> and <strong>Fiddler AI:</strong> These are dedicated machine learning observability platforms that help you monitor, explain, and troubleshoot models in production, with a focus on detecting data drift and other performance issues.</li>
</ul>
<p>If you’ve been struggling to get your AI initiatives off the ground or maintain their performance, it might be time to look beyond the model itself and rebuild trust in your data foundation. Investing in the right tools and practices will not only improve your AI’s accuracy but also foster a culture of data confidence across your entire organization.</p>

33:T17b5,<h3>Guiding Principles</h3>
<h4>Core Philosophy: “Build Up, Don’t Pile On”</h4>
<ul>
<li><strong>Separation of Concerns</strong>: Each model should have a distinct and clear purpose.</li>
<li><strong>Progressive Enrichment</strong>: Introduce complexity incrementally, adding layers as needed.</li>
<li><strong>Domain-Driven Design</strong>: Organise related logic into cohesive groups.</li>
<li><strong>Reusability First</strong>: Prioritise designs that accommodate multiple use cases.</li>
<li><strong>Test at Every Layer</strong>: Validate functionality continuously, rather than waiting until the end.</li>
</ul>
<h3>When to Break Down Models</h3>
<h3>How to Break Down Complex Models</h3>
<p>A common challenge in data engineering is managing complex SQL models with numerous joins and transformations. A single, monolithic model can become a maintenance nightmare, making debugging and updates difficult. The solution is to break down the model into a series of <strong>intermediate models</strong>.</p>
<p>This layered approach offers several benefits:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Clear Data Lineage:</strong> Each model builds logically on the previous one.</li>
<li><strong>Simplified Debugging:</strong> If an issue arises, you can isolate it to a specific layer.</li>
<li><strong>Incremental Development:</strong> You can build and test each layer independently.</li>
<li><strong>Reusability:</strong> A foundational model can be reused by multiple downstream models.</li>
</ul>
<p>Below is a generic refactoring of a complex SQL model, demonstrating how to break it down into a logical sequence of intermediate models.</p>
<h3>1. The Foundation Layer: <code>intermediate_core.sql</code></h3>
<p>This first model establishes the base of your data model. It should include the most critical tables and columns, forming a central “core” dataset that subsequent models will build upon. The goal is to perform only the essential joins and select the foundational data points.</p>
<pre><code>-- This model forms the foundational dataset by selecting core fields
-- from primary staging tables.
{{ config(materialized = &#x27;table&#x27;) }}

with
-- Select from the main subject table
base_subject as (
 select * from {{ ref(&#x27;stg_main_subject_table&#x27;) }}
),
-- Join with a key related table
related_table_a as (
 select * from {{ ref(&#x27;stg_related_table_a&#x27;) }}
),
-- Join with another essential table
related_table_b as (
 select * from {{ ref(&#x27;stg_related_table_b&#x27;) }}
)
select
 -- Core fields from the main table
 base_subject.primary_key,
 base_subject.core_field_1,
 base_subject.core_field_2,
 -- Joining key fields
 related_table_a.lookup_key_a,
 related_table_b.lookup_key_b,
 -- Create a surrogate key for the entity
 {{ dbt_utils.generate_surrogate_key([&#x27;base_subject.primary_key&#x27;]) }} as entity_surrogate_key
from base_subject
left join related_table_a
 on base_subject.common_id = related_table_a.common_id
inner join related_table_b
 on base_subject.another_id = related_table_b.another_id
</code></pre><h3>2. The Enrichment Layer: <code>intermediate_enrichment.sql</code></h3>
<p>This model takes the core dataset from the previous layer and enriches it with additional information from other sources. This is where you would typically join in more granular data or supplementary attributes.</p>
<pre><code>-- This model enriches the core dataset with additional attributes
-- from other staging tables.
{{ config(materialized = &#x27;table&#x27;) }}

with
-- Reference the core model from the previous step
core_data as (
 select * from {{ ref(&#x27;intermediate_core&#x27;) }}
),
-- Select from a table with supplemental data
supplementary_data as (
 select * from {{ ref(&#x27;stg_supplementary_data&#x27;) }}
),
-- Select from a table with dimension data
dimension_data as (
 select * from {{ ref(&#x27;stg_dimension_data&#x27;) }}
)
select
 -- Select all columns from the previous model
 core_data.*,
 -- Add new, enriched fields
 supplementary_data.extra_attribute,
 supplementary_data.derived_field,
 dimension_data.dimension_value
from core_data
left join supplementary_data
 on core_data.primary_key = supplementary_data.primary_key
left join dimension_data
 on core_data.lookup_key_a = dimension_data.lookup_key_a
</code></pre><h3>3. The Final Transformation Layer: <code>final_model.sql</code></h3>
<p>This final model brings everything together. It’s where you apply the last set of business logic, such as <code>CASE</code> statements, data type casting, and final column selection. This layer also handles data lineage and slowly changing dimension (SCD) logic to prepare the data for consumption.</p>
<pre><code>-- This is the final model, where all transformations are complete.
-- It selects and casts the final set of columns for end-user consumption.
{{ config(materialized = &#x27;table&#x27;) }}

with
-- Reference the enriched data from the previous layer
enriched_records as (
 select * from {{ ref(&#x27;intermediate_enrichment&#x27;) }}
),

final_transformations as (
 select
 -- Casting key IDs to appropriate types
 primary_key::varchar(50) as final_entity_id,
 entity_surrogate_key,
 -- Applying business logic with a CASE statement
 case
 when primary_key like &#x27;ABC%&#x27; then &#x27;Type A&#x27;
 else &#x27;Type B&#x27;
 end as entity_type,
 -- Final column selection and aliasing
 core_field_1 as final_attribute_1,
 derived_field as final_attribute_2,
 extra_attribute as final_attribute_3,
 -- Example of SCD Type 2 logic
 effective_start_date as eff_start_dttm,
 case
 when is_deleted_flag then current_timestamp
 else &#x27;9999-12-31 23:59:59&#x27;::timestamp
 end as eff_end_dttm
 from enriched_records
)

-- Final SELECT statement to materialize the model
select * from final_transformations
</code></pre><hr>
